A substantive statutory duty and the Constitution's own prohibition therefore remain distinct even when both protect the same person.

Stone gives Congress substantial latitude to identify patterns, serious risks, methods of evasion, and practical obstacles to proving or remedying constitutional wrongs. Relevant support may include testimony, individual accounts, documents, admissions, circumstantial evidence, historical and institutional experience, comparisons, statistics, practical knowledge, and reasonable predictions grounded in those materials. No universal requirement demands a commissioned study, formal findings, a prescribed quantity or form of evidence, or a proved violation in every covered jurisdiction. Courts must still assess whether the legislative response fits the constitutional injury; the word "enforcement" cannot substitute for that assessment.

For preventive legislation, the relevant fit may concern a class of conduct or an institutional setting. The nature and seriousness of the injury, its likelihood or persistence, barriers to detecting it, the reach of the duty, the remedy, and meaningful statutory safeguards may matter. These are considerations where relevant, not compulsory elements in every case. Geographic limits, sunsets, individualized findings, and exceptions may help establish fit, but none is universally required. Congress may adopt an administrable rule that prevents predictable exclusion without forcing each injured person to reconstruct an institution's entire history of discrimination.

Once a preventive provision is valid, a covered claimant proves its enacted elements. Stone does not add a personal constitutional violation or discriminatory intent that the statute does not require. The justification for Congress's preventive category and the facts needed to establish an individual's statutory claim are separate inquiries. By contrast, when validity rests only on remedying actual constitutional violations, the claimed conduct must meet that constitutional standard. Neither route permits a valid enforcement law to excuse an independently established constitutional wrong. Other Reconstruction powers require attention to their own text and governing law; the Fourteenth Amendment test is not automatically transferred to each of them.

#### Article I alone, Section 5 alone, and both together

From February 21, 2001, Garrett supplies a controlling Title I application of both grounds. Its rejection of categorical state immunity does not decide individual liability, award-specific limits, Title II, or a new constitutional disability tier. Earlier cases use only the law then in force.

Article I alone may resolve the case when it fully supports the duty and enforcement and no material Section 5 question requires decision. Section 5 alone may do so when it fully supports the challenged provision and no sufficient or necessary Article I ground remains to be addressed. Stone decides both when each independently supports the challenged duty and enforcement and both are properly presented and material to disputed coverage, relief or the legal rationale. Neither route requires the other. Success under one creates no automatic reason to avoid a material question under the other. A defect under one does not defeat a genuinely sufficient other ground; two insufficient grounds cannot be combined to manufacture a power neither supplies.

Different provisions or applications may rely on different powers. Article I may support a law's public and private employment coverage while Section 5 independently supports relevant state applications. Each challenged component needs sufficient authority for its duty and enforcement; every component need not satisfy both powers. Combined grounds neither create a second statutory cause of action nor permit duplicate compensation for one injury.

The following examples are hypothetical and assume a proper judicial vehicle and the facts stated.

**Article I alone — unpaid overtime.** A State runs a laboratory and refuses to pay a covered technician overtime required by a valid federal wage law. Assume Congress has unmistakably authorized employees to sue States for the unpaid wages in the court hearing the case. The technician proves the hours worked and the statutory entitlement but alleges no discrimination or other constitutional wrong. Stone would allow the action under Congress's authority to regulate covered employment and enforce that duty. Section 5 is unnecessary. The State owes the wages the employee proves are due, together with any other relief the statute lawfully authorizes; valid congressional power does not itself prove the hours, coverage, or amount owed.

**Section 5 alone — participating in a custody hearing.** A deaf parent cannot understand a state hearing that will decide custody of her child, and the court refuses any effective means of communication. Assume Congress has expressly required effective communication in that setting and authorized a suit to enforce the requirement. No independent Article I connection has been established. Stone would sustain the requirement under Section 5 because it protects the parent's constitutional opportunity to understand and participate in the proceedings affecting her parental rights. On the parent's properly presented accommodation request, the hearing court must provide effective communication before deciding custody through a hearing she cannot meaningfully participate in; it may choose an equally effective lawful method rather than her preferred device. The immediate result is meaningful participation, not delay while the person produces a statistical study. A separate claim for compensation requires statutory authorization, a proper forum, and proof of the injury and causation needed for that remedy.

**Both together — unequal pay at a state hospital.** A state hospital pays a qualified Black nurse less than otherwise comparable white nurses because of race. Assume a federal employment statute validly covers the hospital's economic activity, prohibits that discrimination, and unmistakably authorizes private enforcement against the State. Article I supports regulation of the covered employment and its enforcement; Section 5 independently supports a remedy for the State's intentional denial of equal protection. Stone would permit the claim on both grounds. If the nurse proves those facts, he would order an end to the unlawful pay difference and award the proved lost pay the statute authorizes. Failure to establish one asserted source would require examination of the other, not automatic dismissal. Two valid sources strengthen the legal basis for enforcement but do not permit double recovery for the same loss.

The choice of method follows the question. Constitutional and statutory text and precedent identify the source of power, the protected right and the enacted obligation. A categorical rule is appropriate for a defined prohibition such as the intentional racial exclusion in the hospital example; the government cannot defend that exclusion merely by pointing to administrative convenience. Functional analysis determines whether communication in the custody example actually enables participation. Institutional history, comparisons and practical consequences help explain preventive fit. Where these methods are combined, Stone states which carries decisive weight on the disputed issue. No method receives a general preference, and the reach of the holding follows its legal justification rather than a preference for narrow or broad results.

Tiered scrutiny applies to the underlying classification or right under its governing doctrine. A valid preventive statute may reach conduct beyond that constitutional baseline without changing the tier. Balancing and multifactor standards fit questions of effective accommodation or lawful implementation when function, need, costs and available alternatives materially differ. The opinion identifies the legally relevant considerations and explains their weight. A presumption or burden shift must state its trigger, what rebuts it and what happens if proof fails; it cannot silently add an intent requirement, excuse a required element, or turn every disparity into liability. A safe harbor needs conditions that reliably satisfy the rule, while conduct outside it may still be lawful.

Congress's democratic judgment deserves serious consideration in identifying institutional exclusion and selecting workable preventive protection. Courts retain responsibility for constitutional limits and the legal rights of people whom a majority may disregard. State institutions retain choices among lawful, effective methods where the governing law permits them. History may reveal why a practice denies equal participation; its longevity alone cannot justify the denial. A valid Section 5 duty to correct a state's constitutional failure requires its own analysis and cannot be dismissed merely by equating it with compulsory administration of an unrelated federal program.

#### Distinguish the right from the means of enforcing it

For a private suit, Stone identifies the right-holder, duty, authorized action, defendant, jurisdiction, causation, and relief. The statute's text, structure, and remedial scheme govern. A substantive violation alone does not establish a private action, and recognizing an action does not automatically authorize every kind of damages. A constitutional source for the legislation likewise does not supply a cause of action omitted from the applicable law.

Section 1983 supplies an enacted enforcement route where its conditions are met; it is not the same inquiry as implying a new cause of action directly from another statute. Stone's approach recognizes that a statute can confer an enforceable individual right while also directing an agency to oversee a program. Administrative supervision alone does not necessarily displace private enforcement. Conversely, a general program instruction does not become an individual entitlement merely because a person benefits from compliance. Rights-conferring language, the intended holder of the right, the obligation's content, and the compatibility of private enforcement with the enacted remedial scheme require actual analysis.

Regulations may implement authority that legislation actually confers; they do not create otherwise missing power or eliminate statutory limits. Whether a regulation specifies the content of an already enforceable right or imposes a distinct obligation without the asserted private remedy depends on the enactment and governing precedent.

The tracker records that Stone joined the fictional *Powell v. Ridge* holding denying the private regulatory disparate-impact action, while an earlier *Sandoval* plan would allow it. His willingness to distinguish or change *Powell* is settled. The later opinion must identify a material legal distinction if one exists; if the same rule controls and he concludes it is wrong, he will argue for narrowing or overruling it. His earlier vote is part of the explanation, not a bar to changing his position. The remaining work is to establish the statutory reasoning, the scope of the change, and its consequences.

A law directed at private conduct needs a source capable of reaching that conduct. Where a claim or power requires state action, misconduct does not become governmental merely because it is grave, widespread, regulated, or publicly funded. Governmental control, attribution, delegated public functions, joint action, and institutional entwinement must be analyzed under their actual doctrines. Appropriately adapted enforcement of a State's constitutional obligations must be distinguished from treating every private wrong as a State violation. Other powers and statutes may regulate private discrimination without converting it into constitutional state action.

#### Immunity and the proper defendant

Immunity determines whether this defendant can face this claim for this relief in this forum. It does not make the challenged conduct lawful. Where sovereign immunity applies, Stone requires an applicable waiver or a constitutionally valid congressional removal of immunity with the necessary clarity. An unmistakably authorized state suit may rest on valid Article I regulation and enforcement, valid Section 5 legislation, or both. The scope of the authorization, the forum, and the remedy must be established rather than inferred from the existence of a federal duty.

An ongoing violation may support authorized prospective relief against a responsible official with a real enforcement connection. That route does not automatically authorize retrospective treasury damages. Public enforcement by the United States, state consent, statutory funding conditions, and claims against local bodies have their own rules. Ordinary participation in commerce is not itself consent to suit. A court must not collapse these distinct grounds into a universal conclusion that government either can or cannot be sued.

Personal defenses, institutional attribution, and the availability of monetary relief must likewise be identified under the applicable law. The seriousness of an injury does not supply a missing damages action or erase a defense. Once a lawful channel exists, Stone favors relief that actually addresses the established violation. Compensation responds to proved loss; prospective relief prevents or ends the unlawful barrier. The court should explain the conditions for each instead of treating one as an automatic substitute for the other.

#### Funding conditions, fees, and remedial limits

Funding conditions need the clarity, statutory authority, connection, and constitutional validity that the governing doctrine requires. A practically coercive arrangement is not made voluntary by its contractual label. Notice of the duty and notice of a particular remedy may be separate issues. Acceptance of funds on a valid express condition may establish consent within that condition's coverage; it does not establish a general waiver for unrelated claims or remedies.

Stone's planned position in *Barnes* distinguishes authorized compensation for proved injury from punitive damages that the provisions at issue do not authorize. The limitation on punishment does not diminish the substantive access right. His planned position in *Buckhannon* favors catalyst fees where a nonfrivolous, legally grounded suit materially caused the relief, with the applicable fee statute and causal requirements identified. These proposed interpretations require their own legal justification and express treatment of any controlling contrary rule.

Practical burdens belong in the inquiry the law makes relevant. An institution may establish actual costs, capacity limits, or a defense the governing law recognizes through appropriate evidence, including experience and a concrete operational explanation. An employment provision's undue-hardship or job-related screening defense must not be replaced with a fundamental-alteration defense belonging to a different statutory setting. Neither a bare assertion of expense nor the absence of a formal study decides the issue. Where the statute permits equally effective methods, institutions retain that choice; where immediate action is necessary to protect a right, routine administrative convenience does not justify an ineffective remedy. Courts must respect lawful remedial limits while explaining what relief will make the established protection effective.

### Foundation 17. Using the foundation to infer future dispositions

The foundation is sufficient for educated character inference across the tracker’s subjects. It cannot predetermine every future judgment without the statute, case posture, record, and controlling fictional decisions. That is a limit of adjudication, not a need to collect a new general statement of Stone’s values for each case.

For a proposed opinion, record:

1. **The law at the date of the case.** Identify controlling fictional decisions, the enacted text then in force, and any preserved reservation. Do not import later research or legislation.
2. **Stone’s applicable commitment.** Point to a stated rule in this foundation. Identify whether the proposed application is expressly settled here, a strong inference, or a genuinely open choice.
3. **The reasoning that connects the two.** Explain the relevant legal analogy, comparator, institutional function, factual premise, or source of authority. Give the strongest lawful opposing argument a real answer.
4. **The likely disposition.** State affirmance, reversal, vacatur, remand, dismissal, or a narrower partial disposition, and identify which legal or factual condition supports it. A favorable view of a right does not automatically imply judgment for the claimant on this record.
5. **The role of his opinion.** Distinguish what he could join under existing law, what he would add in concurrence, and what would require dissent or an express proposal to change precedent. His preference does not establish other Justices’ votes.
6. **Relief and limits.** State the authorized consequence and the questions not decided. Do not use a narrow remedy to conceal a change in the underlying rule.

An educated inference should be conditional where a missing fact could change the result: “Stone would likely reject this exclusion if the asserted operational difference is unsupported, but would remand if that material factual issue was never properly resolved.” It should not invent the needed facts, attribute an unrecorded vote, or disguise a proposed legal rule as settled law.

The latest voting answers settle the preferred protection for intentional fragmentation and the refusal to exchange reliable electoral opportunity for merely diffuse influence. The optional use of many alternative maps and support for transparency are interpreted consistently with the existing rejection of universal procedures. They do not establish a simulation prerequisite or a new racial-design-only claim.

Several disputes still require legal analysis in their actual cases. These include the basis for distinguishing or changing *Powell* in *Sandoval*, the boundary between some *Miller/Vera* statements about standing and merits, the precise constitutional standard for particular enforcement legislation, and statutory questions not answered by personal support for protection. Stone’s willingness to act is not an open character question. The analysis must determine and defend the legal route, rather than treat the presence of contrary precedent as a reason to stop.

For territorial standing, how should personal exposure to the challenged assignment connect to the specific injury claimed, while keeping standing distinct from whether racial district design itself violates equal protection? Any reconciliation must address the actual holdings and Stone’s earlier writings. The proposed application belongs in the planning section and does not record a correction already adopted by the Court.

## 1. Completed cases

OT1991

- Campos v. City of Houston (application)
  > Held (unsigned order, 9-0 and 6-3): The Court denied an injunction stopping Houston's November 1991 city election (9-0) and denied the alternative stay of the district court's order requiring Plan 2 (6-3), stating no rationale. The order decided no doctrine and left Plan 2 in place.
- Norman v. Reed
  > Held (Souter for 7): Illinois may require candidates to show support within each separate electorate, but may not bar authorized candidates from extending an existing party under its name, or disqualify qualified countywide and city candidates because candidates in another district lacked signatures. The completed election did not moot the dispute.
- Presley v. Etowah County Commission
  > Held (Kennedy for 5): Section 5 did not require preclearance of two Alabama counties' reallocations of road authority among elected commissioners, because the changes had no direct relation to voting. Whether an extreme transfer could effectively replace an elected office with an appointed one was left open.
- United States Department of Commerce v. Montana
  > Held (Stevens for the Court, 9-0): The equal-proportions method of allocating House seats among the States satisfies Article I and the Fourteenth Amendment, and the challenge is justiciable. An alternative that improves some measures of equality while worsening others does not show a violation.
- Freeman v. Pitts
  > Held (Stone for the Court, 8-0): A court may release completed parts of a school-desegregation decree only after full and satisfactory compliance and elimination of the vestiges there to the extent practicable, with good faith shown toward the whole decree. The district must show that remaining imbalance is not traceable to the violation.
- Trevino v. Texas
  > Held (per curiam, 9-0): A pre-Batson motion alleging a pattern of racial peremptory strikes, renewed and carried forward on appeal, preserves the equal-protection objection. Batson applies to cases still on direct review, and the defendant need not share the excluded jurors' race.
- Burdick v. Takushi
  > Held (White for the Court, 5-4): Election rules are reviewed by weighing the burden on voting rights against the State's precise interests: severe burdens must be narrowly drawn to a compelling interest, while reasonable nondiscriminatory rules are usually justified by important regulatory interests. Hawaii's write-in ban was valid within its ballot-access system.
- Burlington Northern Railroad Co. v. Ford
  > Held (Souter for 8, judgment 9-0): Montana's venue rule, which lets plaintiffs choose any county when suing a corporation incorporated elsewhere while resident corporations keep their home county, passes ordinary rational-basis review as applied to a railroad incorporated in Delaware and headquartered in Texas.
- Nordlinger v. Hahn
  > Held (Blackmun for 6): California's acquisition-value property tax, with capped increases and its over-55 and parent-child exceptions, rationally serves legitimate interests such as neighborhood continuity and satisfies equal protection. A taxpayer alleging no burden on her own travel cannot invoke absent travelers' rights to obtain heightened review.
- Georgia v. McCollum
  > Held (Blackmun for 5): A criminal defendant's purposeful racial peremptory strikes are state action and violate the excluded jurors' equal-protection rights, and the State may object on their behalf. The ordinary Batson sequence governs, with the burden of persuasion on the State.
- United States v. Fordice
  > Held (White for the Court, 8-1): A State that ran a segregated university system must eliminate traceable policies that still foster segregation where that can practicably be done consistently with sound educational practice. Good-faith neutral policies are not enough, and the State bears the burden of showing it has dismantled the dual system.
- Franklin v. Massachusetts
  > Held (Stone for the Court, 9-0): The constitutional census count may include federal personnel temporarily overseas who have enduring residence ties to a State. Five Justices also held the Secretary's completed census decision reviewable under the APA and not arbitrary on this record.

OT1992

- Growe v. Emison
  > Held (Scalia for the Court, 9-0): A federal court must defer to a State's legislature or courts when they are timely redistricting, unless they will not produce a lawful plan in time. A §2 claim that single-member districts fragment a minority group requires proof of all three Gingles conditions.
- Voinovich v. Quilter
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 9-0): Section 2 does not make a State prove a federal violation before it draws majority-minority districts; challengers must prove unequal opportunity. The finding of intentional discrimination was clearly erroneous, and population deviations over ten percent require the State's justification.
- FCC v. Beach Communications, Inc.
  > Held (Thomas for 8): An economic classification touching no suspect class or fundamental right stands if any reasonably conceivable legitimate basis supports it; the challenger must negate it, and no legislative findings are needed. Congress could rationally treat commonly owned buildings differently in cable regulation.
- Northeastern Florida Chapter, AGC v. Jacksonville
  > Held (Thomas for the Court, 7-2): Contractors able and ready to bid may challenge a racial set-aside without proving they would have won a contract, because denial of equal opportunity to compete is itself the injury. Replacing the ordinance with a similar one did not moot the case.
- Heller v. Doe
  > Held (Souter for 5; Kennedy for 6 on family participation): Kentucky's lower burden of proof for committing adults with intellectual disability than adults with mental illness fails rational-basis review. Letting relatives and guardians take part as parties is rational and does not violate due process.
- Shaw v. Reno
  > Held (Stone for the Court, 5-4): Deliberate or predominant use of race in drawing territorial districts does not by itself state an equal-protection claim; racial exclusion, intentional dilution, materially unequal participation and inferior civic status remain actionable. The dismissal of the claims against federal officials was left undisturbed, 9-0.

OT1993

- J.E.B. v. Alabama ex rel. T.B.
  > Held (Blackmun for the Court, 7-2): The State may not strike jurors because of sex; an assumption that men will favor the alleged father fails the requirement that a sex classification substantially serve an important objective. Objections follow a staged inquiry of prima facie showing, sex-neutral explanation and decision.
- Holder v. Hall
  > Held (Blackmun for the Court, 5-4): The size of an elected county governing body is covered by §2 (six votes), and a challenge may use a reasonable, workable comparator rooted in the actual legal setting, here a locally authorized five-member board. Full Gingles and totality proof is still required; liability and the remedy remand were affirmed.
- Johnson v. De Grandy
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 7-2): The Gingles conditions are necessary but not sufficient, §2 imposes no duty to maximize minority districts, and proportionality is relevant evidence rather than a safe harbor. Florida House liability was reversed and the Senate judgment affirmed.

OT1994

- Anderson v. Green
  > Held (per curiam, 9-0): A constitutional challenge to a benefit program was not ripe once the federal approval the program depended on had been removed. The lower judgments were vacated with directions to dismiss without prejudice.
- U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton
  > Held (Stevens for the Court, 6-3): A State may not deny ballot access to otherwise qualified congressional candidates because of prior congressional service, because that adds a qualification to the exclusive ones in Article I.
- Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 9-0): Title IX guarantees no particular team, and effective accommodation may be shown by any one of three routes. A public university may consider sex in restructuring only for an actual important objective that the choice substantially serves; ratios, budget pressure or attempted compliance alone are not enough.
- Missouri v. Jenkins
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 5-4): A desegregation court may fund salaries reasonably necessary to deliver educational measures that repair segregation-caused injury. Partial release requires the full Freeman showing, which Missouri had not made.
- Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña
  > Held (Stone for 5, judgment 9-0): A governmental racial classification affecting competitive opportunity requires an important objective within lawful authority, a substantial evidence-supported relationship to it, and justified coverage and burdens, including an answer to concrete practicable alternatives. The judgment was vacated and remanded to apply this common standard.
- Miller v. Johnson (Johnson v. Miller)
  > Held (Stone for the Court, 5-4): Predominant racial consideration in drawing a shared territorial district does not by itself violate equal protection or trigger strict scrutiny. All nine agreed that voters personally assigned to the district have standing and that a State's voluntary inclusion is not unconstitutional merely because no federal law requires it.

OT1995

- Klinger v. Department of Corrections
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 9-0): Prisoners' similarity is judged program by program; an actual sex classification needs an important objective and a substantial relationship, while a neutral program choice requires proof of purpose. The liability finding was reversed and the categorical dismissal vacated.
- Southern Christian Leadership Conference of Alabama v. Sessions
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 5-4): Section 2 covers at-large judicial elections; a State's interest in linking judges' electorates to their jurisdictions weighs heavily but does not categorically defeat a proved violation or every remedy. The judgment was vacated for reconsideration.
- Voting Rights Coalition v. Wilson
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 9-0): The Elections Clause authorizes the NVRA's registration duties for congressional elections, and Congress's protective power supports them for presidential elections. The Attorney General's compliance injunction against California was affirmed.
- Wisconsin v. City of New York
  > Held (Stevens for the Court, 9-0): The Secretary's explained decision not to adjust the 1990 census bore the required reasonable relationship to an actual enumeration and was constitutional, even though another method might improve some measures of accuracy.
- Ayers v. Fordice
  > Held (8-1): The writ was dismissed as improvidently granted, leaving the Mississippi higher-education remedial judgment in place without affirming any provision on its merits.
- Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 6-3): A changed fee to take part in a party convention that chose the party's ballot nominee was subject to §5 preclearance, and affected voters may bring a private action under §10 against an unlawful poll tax. Whether the fee was unlawful was left open.
- United States v. Armstrong
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 7-2): Discovery on a racial selective-prosecution claim requires some credible evidence of discriminatory effect and purpose, including a reliable basis to infer that similarly situated people of another race could have been prosecuted but were not (eight votes). That is less than a prima facie case.
- Romer v. Evans
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 7-2): Colorado's Amendment 2, which withdrew orientation-based antidiscrimination protection and barred restoring it through ordinary lawmaking, fails ordinary rational-basis review because its class-specific disability bears no rational relation to legitimate interests.
- Bush v. Vera
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 5-4; Thomas for the eight Associates on standing): Racial predominance in a shared territorial district does not alone establish an equal-protection violation or trigger strict scrutiny, so the Texas invalidation was reversed. Voters personally assigned to the challenged districts had standing (9-0).
- United States v. Virginia
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 7-1): Virginia did not show a genuine important objective substantially served by excluding women from VMI, and the separate VWIL program did not cure the denied opportunity. Virginia must promptly admit women on lawful terms or provide an actually adequate alternative.

OT1996

- Lopez v. Monterey County (1996)
  > Held (Kennedy for the Court, 9-0): A covered county must preclear voting changes it administers, even when a noncovered State compels them. A court may not authorize an unprecleared plan by calling it court ordered; only a necessary temporary plan wholly formulated by the court under independent authority escapes preclearance.
- Hopwood v. Texas
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court; 9-0 on the 1992 process, six votes on diversity): The law school's separate racial admissions process fails Adarand's common standard. A supported interest in the concrete educational benefits of a genuinely diverse student body can be an important objective.
- M.L.B. v. S.L.J.
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 7-2): When a State provides a first appeal from a decree permanently terminating parental rights, it may not deny an indigent parent that appeal because she cannot prepay for the record.
- Walmer v. Department of Defense
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 5-4): A conduct-based military personnel rule receives ordinary rational-basis review, which sustained denial of a preliminary injunction on a preliminary record of admitted homosexual acts. The attainder claim failed 9-0 because an executive regulation is not legislative action.
- General Motors Corp. v. Tracy
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 7-2): Ohio may tax independent natural-gas marketers' sales while exempting regulated local distribution companies, whose rate and service duties rationally distinguish them for equal protection; the record showed no Commerce Clause discrimination.
- Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center
  > Held (Breyer for the Court, 9-0): A hospital rule keeping spouses from working together on an emergency-flight team burdens marriage only incidentally, not directly and substantially, and satisfies rational-basis review.
- Nabozny v. Podlesny
  > Held (Stone for the Court, 9-0): Officials who intentionally deny a student ordinary school protection because he is male must meet intermediate scrutiny, and purposeful, irrational denial because of sexual orientation violates equal protection under rational-basis review. Qualified immunity did not shield the officials.
- Young v. Fordice
  > Held (Stevens for the Court, 9-0): Discretionary state changes made while implementing the NVRA must be precleared under §5. A briefly used provisional plan never became the benchmark; the old system was.
- Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party
  > Held (Thomas for the Court, 5-4): Minnesota may bar a minor party from nominating a candidate who also seeks or holds a major party's nomination; the burden is less than severe and justified by interests in ballot integrity and a stable electoral system.
- Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board (Bossier I)
  > Held (Breyer for the Court, 9-0 on vacatur): Section 5's effect test asks only about retrogression, and six held that a §2 violation is not itself a ground to deny preclearance. Five held that §5 also bars a change adopted with a purpose to dilute minority votes, even without intended retrogression.
- Thomasson v. Perry
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 5-4): Under ordinary rational-basis review, the Navy may treat a voluntary statement of homosexual identity as a genuinely rebuttable indication of prohibited conduct or propensity, and using the statement as evidence does not violate the First Amendment.
- Piscataway Township Board of Education v. Taxman
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 5-4): Choosing which of two equally qualified, equally senior employees to lay off by race solely to preserve departmental diversity violates Title VII. A constitutional diversity interest creates no Title VII exemption (9-0).
- Cohen v. Brown University
  > Held (Scalia for the Court, 9-0): Title IX's three athletics accommodation routes are independent alternatives, and the sex-conscious enforcement here satisfies intermediate scrutiny. After a violation, the institution first chooses among effective lawful remedies.
- Lawyer v. Department of Justice
  > Held (Breyer for the Court, 8-1): A districting consent decree approved on the premise that a racial-predominance claim was fairly contestable must be reconsidered, because governing precedent rejects that claim. A voter personally assigned under the decree may seek review.
- Vacco v. Quill
  > Held (Thomas for 5, judgment 9-0): New York may permit refusal of medical treatment while prohibiting assisted suicide; the distinction rests on consent, causation and intent and satisfies rational-basis review.
- Foreman v. Dallas County
  > Held (per curiam, 9-0): A covered county's changed method of appointing precinct election officials requires §5 preclearance even if a precleared state statute gave it discretion. The case was remanded to identify the 1972 baseline practice.

OT1997

- City of Monroe v. United States
  > Held (per curiam, 7-2): A §5 enforcement court may decide what an approved submission covers. Louisiana's precleared statewide majority-vote default independently governed Monroe, whose charter was silent, so the injunction requiring plurality voting was reversed.
- Foster v. Love
  > Held (Ginsburg for the Court, 9-0): Louisiana may not conclusively elect a member of Congress in October, leaving nothing to decide on the federal election day; that conflicts with the federal election-day statutes.
- Richenberg v. Cohen
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 5-4): Under ordinary rationality review, a statement-based military discharge was sustained after a genuine opportunity to rebut the conduct-related inference; meaningful consideration of rebuttal does not require accepting every denial.
- Valot v. Southeast Local School District Board of Education
  > Held (Souter for the Court; 9-0 on the equality claim, 6-3 on the petition claim): A classification based on employees' benefit applications rationally relates to possible fiscal costs and does not deny equal protection. A public employer may not refuse continued employment solely because an employee lawfully seeks unemployment benefits.
- Shahar v. Bowers
  > Held (Souter for 5): An adverse civilian employment decision is not exempt from ordinary equal-protection review because it is called conduct-based or a hiring choice; the actual disadvantage must rationally relate to a legitimate interest, with no heightened tier for orientation. Summary judgment was vacated and remanded.
- Texas v. United States
  > Held (Stevens for the Court, 9-0): Texas's request to exempt possible future school-district interventions from §5 was unripe because no specific intervention had been defined.
- Campbell v. Louisiana
  > Held (Breyer for the Court, 9-0): A white defendant may challenge racial discrimination in choosing a voting member of his grand jury as his own due-process injury. Six Justices held he may also assert the excluded Black citizens' equal-protection rights.
- Miller v. Albright
  > Held (no opinion of the Court, 5-4): The denial of citizenship to the child of an unmarried citizen father was affirmed without any controlling rationale on the sex-based conditions or the remedy. Six Justices agreed the child could assert the father's equality claim.
- City of Florence v. Chipman / Stemler v. City of Florence
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 9-0): Probable cause does not defeat an equal-protection claim that officers deliberately singled out a chargeable driver out of arbitrary group hostility while leaving a comparable driver alone; Stemler's claim proceeds without any orientation-based tier. A bounded due-process claim lies for knowingly forcing a person into appreciated danger.
- Duffy v. Wolle
  > Held (Stevens for the Court; 9-0 on these points, 8-1 on the mandate): The judiciary's internal EEO plan does not displace a federal applicant's Fifth Amendment sex-discrimination damages action under Davis v. Passman, and judges have no absolute immunity for hiring a chief probation officer.
- Engineering Contractors Association of South Florida v. Metropolitan Dade County
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court; 8-1 on race, 5-4 on sex): Local racial contracting preferences must meet Adarand's common standard, including serious consideration of race-neutral alternatives, which Dade County had not shown. The women's program failed intermediate scrutiny for lack of substantiated gender exclusion.
- Libertarian Party of Florida v. Smith
  > Held (Stevens for the Court, 8-1): Florida may allocate a limited party-expense benefit by an objective five-percent registration criterion where the burden on association is not severe.
- Libertarian Party of Illinois v. Rednour
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 8-1): Under Anderson and Burdick, Illinois's congressional ballot-access requirements stand where the whole system leaves a reasonable route to the ballot. Genuine nomination procedures add no qualification for Congress (9-0).

OT1998

- Fraternal Order of Police v. United States (1998)
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 9-0): Congress may withhold the governmental-use exception from the domestic-violence-misdemeanor firearm provisions while keeping it for the felony provision; equal protection does not require it to perfect older exemptions at the same time.
- Hiley v. Barrett
  > Held (O'Connor for 8, judgment 9-0): Congress may restrict firearms after a domestic-violence misdemeanor without extending the rule to every violent misdemeanor. Differing state restoration systems, and heavier effects on armed occupations without a discriminatory purpose, do not deny equal protection.
- Peoples Rights Organization v. City of Columbus
  > Held (Thomas for the Court; 7 on the equality point): Columbus may exempt weapons registered under its former ordinance from its assault-weapons ban, because protecting documented registration reliance is rational. Two handgun "modification" definitions were invalid for failing to identify the prohibited relationship, and the other categorical invalidations were reversed.
- Coalition for Economic Equity v. Wilson
  > Held (Breyer for the Court, 5-4): A State's selective transfer of authority over otherwise lawful racial remedies to a more remote political process (here Proposition 209) requires the political-process inquiry. The sex theory needs its own inquiry, and Title VII does not categorically preempt the measure (9-0).
- Equality Foundation of Greater Cincinnati v. City of Cincinnati
  > Held (Ginsburg for the Court, 7-2): Cincinnati's charter amendment withdrawing sexual-orientation protection and barring ordinary municipal protection for that class fails rational-basis review, and injunctive protection against it was restored.
- Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation
  > Held (Stevens for the Court; 7-2, and 9-0 on badges): On this record Colorado may not require initiative petition circulators to be registered voters, to wear personal name badges, or to have their identities and pay published in individual reports.
- Lopez v. Monterey County (1999)
  > Held (Thomas for the Court, 9-0): A covered county must obtain preclearance before administering a voting change that a noncovered State mandates, even when the county has no discretion. Delay does not authorize use of an unprecleared change.
- Citizens for a Constitutional Convention v. Yoshina
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 9-0): A state court's neutral reading of an ambiguous referendum rule does not violate due process merely because officials earlier advised otherwise, and counting blank ballots in the approval denominator compels no speech.
- Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives / Clinton v. Glavin
  > Held (Souter for the Court; Glavin 5-4, House 7-2): The Census Act and the Enumeration Clause permit the two proposed statistical methods as part of an actual enumeration used for apportionment. The House lacked standing, and its suit was dismissed.
- Hart v. Gwadosky
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 9-0): The challenge became moot once the initiative qualified for the ballot, and the Maine judgment was vacated for further state proceedings.
- Central State University v. AAUP
  > Held (per curiam, 8-1): Ohio may exclude public-university faculty workloads from collective bargaining to promote classroom teaching; an ordinary occupational classification need only rationally serve a legitimate purpose, without empirical proof.
- Brower v. Washington
  > Held (Kennedy for the Court, 9-0): A legislature may refer a measure on condition that a private beneficiary reimburse election costs; the condition charged no one to vote, gave the beneficiary no vote, and created no right to delay or equalize the campaign.
- Brooks v. Barnes
  > Held (Souter for the Court; 6-3 on §2, 9-0 on the Constitution): A §2 challenge to a primary runoff requirement turns on equal opportunity in the nomination process, not proof that minority-preferred nominees would win in November. The constitutional claim failed on supported findings of no discriminatory purpose.
- Saenz v. Roe
  > Held (Kennedy for the Court, 8-1): A citizen who becomes a bona fide resident must receive equal ordinary welfare benefits; California's first-year cap at the former State's level violates the Fourteenth Amendment's Citizenship and Privileges or Immunities Clauses, and Congress cannot authorize it.
- Hunt v. Cromartie
  > Held (Ginsburg for the Court; 9-0 on vacatur, 5-4 on the rule): A districting injunction cannot rest solely on a finding that race predominated; plaintiffs must prove a separately cognizable denial of equal participation or treatment. A genuine race-versus-politics dispute cannot be resolved on summary judgment when motive is material.
- Maine Green Party v. Maine
  > Held (no opinion of the Court, 5-4): The rejection of the party's facial challenge to Maine's presidential-vote condition was affirmed without a majority rationale. Five Justices agreed that sharpening a legal organizational-autonomy argument does not forfeit it.
- Valdespino v. Alamo Heights Independent School District
  > Held (Stone for the Court, 5-4): The first Gingles condition can be met by a district majority or by rigorous proof that a lawful, compact district would let a cohesive group with dependable crossover support elect its candidates. Citizen voting-age shares below 50 percent neither prove nor foreclose that.
- City of Dallas v. Dallas Fire Fighters Association
  > Held (Kennedy for the Court; 5-4 on race, 9-0 on sex): Municipal racial promotion preferences must meet Adarand's common standard, and sex preferences intermediate scrutiny. Dallas's racial preferences lacked a connected justification and fit, and its sex preferences lacked a supported sex-specific justification.

OT1999

- Texas v. Lesage
  > Held (per curiam, 9-0): Once an unconstitutional admissions process is shown, the school can defeat relief for the rejection itself by proving a constitutional process would have produced the same decision. That does not erase the separate unequal-process injury, which may support nominal or declaratory relief.
- Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Slater
  > Held (per curiam, 9-0): Adarand's later state certification did not moot its challenge, because the government had not shown that the challenged disadvantage could not reasonably recur.
- Gutierrez v. Ada
  > Held (Souter for the Court, 9-0): Under the Guam Organic Act, a gubernatorial majority is measured against votes cast for Governor and Lieutenant Governor; ballots expressing no choice in that contest are excluded, so no runoff was required.
- Rayphand v. Sablan / Torres v. Sablan
  > Held (Kennedy for the Court, 8-1): Congress's expressly protected settlement for the Commonwealth of the Northern Mariana Islands may keep one legislative chamber with equal representation for each municipality alongside a population-based chamber, although an ordinary State legislature could not.
- Fraternal Order of Police v. United States (2000)
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 9-0): The ban on firearm possession by persons convicted of domestic-violence misdemeanors is within the commerce power as part of the valid supply regime, and applying it to state and local officers does not commandeer the States.
- Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board (Bossier II)
  > Held (Breyer for the Court, 5-4): Section 5 bars a change adopted with an unconstitutional purpose to dilute minority votes even without intended retrogression, and the Board had not shown the absence of that purpose, so preclearance of its 1992 plan was denied. The controversy remained live.
- Rice v. Cayetano
  > Held (Thomas for the Court, 7-2): A State may not restrict the electorate for a public state office by racial ancestry; Hawaii's ancestry qualification for voting for Office of Hawaiian Affairs trustees violates the Fifteenth Amendment.
- Village of Willowbrook v. Olech
  > Held (Stevens for the Court, 9-0): An individual states an equal-protection claim by alleging intentional treatment different from similarly situated persons without a rational basis; membership in a group is unnecessary, and ill will is not an element.
- Powell v. Ridge
  > Held (Thomas for the Court, 5-4): Title VI implies no private action to enforce a §602 regulation's disparate-impact prohibition, and the regulation creates no individual right enforceable under §1983.
- Reform Party of Allegheny County v. Allegheny County Department of Elections
  > Held (Breyer for the Court; 9-0 on the rule, 5-4 on relief): Where Pennsylvania lets major parties cross-nominate for certain offices, it may not reject a qualifying minor party's pre-primary nomination solely because the candidate also filed for or won major-party primaries, without a sufficient justification for the unequal treatment. Relief must be confined to that violation.
- Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools
  > Held (Stone for the Court, 7-2): A school's racial transfer classification must meet Adarand's common standard, and six Justices held that the supported educational benefits of K-12 integration and of avoiding serious racial isolation can be an important objective. The policy's validity was left for remand.
- Mixon v. Ohio
  > Held (Souter for the Court; six on §2 coverage, five for reversal): Section 2 can reach a direct replacement of an existing election by appointment, with the former system as the comparison. Residents outside Cleveland have no right to vote for its mayor because he appoints their school board (9-0).
- California Democratic Party v. Jones
  > Held (O'Connor for the Court, 7-2): California's blanket primary, which forced objecting parties to give nonmembers an equal role in choosing their official nominees, severely burdens association and is not justified by the State's asserted interests.

## 2. Reserved landmark cases

Questions are quoted from the Supreme Court's Questions Presented documents (supremecourt.gov/qp) for OT2002 onward, and from the Court's opinions for the four OT2000 cases, whose question documents are not online. Where the Court limited or rewrote the question, the granted question is quoted. Long factual preambles are omitted and marked.

Case-specific recommendations and their supporting reasoning are collected only in [section 6](#voting-plan). Blank recommendation fields and older answers have been removed from this question register.

Status key: Open (the central question has not been reached); Partly decided (an earlier decision of this Court answered part of it); Pre-decided (an earlier decision of this Court answered it, or an earlier case chosen to decide it will); Predicate removed (the question assumes a rule this timeline rejected, so the case returns only in a changed form or is deleted under the causal rule).

### OT2000: Bush v. Palm Beach County Canvassing Board, 531 U.S. 70 (No. 00-836)

Area: Franchise and counting.
Status: Open. On the OT2000 list (December 4, 2000).
Question, as stated in the Court's opinion:
> We granted certiorari on two of the questions presented by petitioner: whether the decision of the Florida Supreme Court, by effectively changing the State's elector appointment procedures after election day, violated the Due Process Clause or 3 U.S.C. §5, and whether the decision of that court changed the manner in which the State's electors are to be selected, in violation of the legislature's power to designate the manner for selection under Art. II, §1, cl. 2 of the United States Constitution.

### OT2000: Bush v. Gore, 531 U.S. 98 (No. 00-949)

Area: Franchise and counting.
Status: Open. On the OT2000 list (December 12, 2000).
Question, as stated in the Court's opinion:
> The petition presents the following questions: whether the Florida Supreme Court established new standards for resolving Presidential election contests, thereby violating Art. II, §1, cl. 2, of the United States Constitution and failing to comply with 3 U.S.C. § 5 and whether the use of standardless manual recounts violates the Equal Protection and Due Process Clauses.

### OT2000: Easley v. Cromartie, 532 U.S. 234 (Nos. 99-1864, 99-1865)

Area: Territorial districting.
Status: Predicate changed. Historical Easley reviewed a post-trial finding that race predominated; Hunt v. Cromartie (OT1998) forbids relief on predominance alone. Its posture must be reconstructed for this timeline before it is decided. On the OT2000 list (April 18, 2001).
Question, as stated in the Court's opinion:
> The issue in this case is evidentiary. We must determine whether there is adequate support for the District Court's key findings, particularly the ultimate finding that the legislature's motive was predominantly racial, not political.

### OT2000: Tuan Anh Nguyen v. INS, 533 U.S. 53 (No. 99-2071)

Area: Sex.
Status: Open. It is to be decided in full, including the remedy, so Morales-Santana (OT2016) becomes an application. On the OT2000 list (June 11, 2001).
Question, as stated in the Court's opinion:
> The question before us is whether the statutory distinction is consistent with the equal protection guarantee embedded in the Due Process Clause of the Fifth Amendment.

### OT2002: Grutter v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 306 (No. 02-241)

Area: Race (admissions).
Status: Partly decided. Hopwood Pt II holds that a supported educational-diversity interest can qualify under the common standard. Open: method, degree, duration, deference.
> 1. Does the University of Michigan Law School's use of racial preferences in student admissions violate the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment, Title VI of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 (42 U.S.C. § 2000d), or 42 U.S.C. § 1981?
> 2. Should an appellate court required to apply strict scrutiny to governmental race-based preferences review de novo the district court's findings because the fact issues are "constitutional"?

### OT2002: Gratz v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 244 (No. 02-516)

Area: Race (admissions).
Status: Partly decided, as Grutter.
> 1. Does the University of Michigan's use of racial preferences in undergraduate admissions violate the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment, Title VI of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 (42 U.S.C. § 2000d), or 42 U.S.C. § 1981?
> 2. Did the district court correctly dismiss the plaintiff class's claim for injunctive relief with respect to the University of Michigan's use of racial preferences in undergraduate admissions?
> 3. Did the individual defendants violate the clearly established legal rights of plaintiffs, so that they are not entitled to summary judgment on the ground of qualified immunity?

### OT2002: Georgia v. Ashcroft, 539 U.S. 461 (No. 02-182)

Area: VRA §5.
Status: Open. Valdespino's ability-to-elect focus frames it.
> I. Whether Section 5 of the Voting Rights Act requires the drawing of safe majority-minority districts with supermajority minority populations, rather than districts that afford minorities equal opportunities at success?
> II. Whether Section 5 can be constitutionally construed to require the drawing of supermajority minority legislative districts in order to create safe seats, rather than seats that afford minorities equal opportunities at success?
> III. Whether private parties should be allowed to intervene in a section 5 preclearance action and assume the role and authority of the Attorney General?

### OT2002: Lawrence v. Texas, 539 U.S. 558 (No. 02-102)

Area: Sexual orientation.
Status: Open.
> 1. Whether Petitioners' criminal convictions under the Texas "Homosexual Conduct" law - which criminalizes sexual intimacy by same-sex couples, but not identical behavior by different-sex couples - violate the Fourteenth Amendment guarantee of equal protection of the laws?
> 2. Whether Petitioners' criminal convictions for adult consensual sexual intimacy in the home violate their vital interests in liberty and privacy protected by the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment?
> 3. Whether Bowers v. Hardwick, 478 U.S. 186 (1986), should be overruled?

### OT2003: Vieth v. Jubelirer, 541 U.S. 267 (No. 02-1580)

Area: Representation (partisan districting).
Status: Open.
> 1. Whether the District Court erred in effectively concluding that voters affiliated with a major political party may never state a claim of unconstitutional partisan gerrymandering, thereby nullifying this Court's decision in Davis v. Bandemer, 478 U.S. 109 (1986).
> 2. Whether a State presumptively violates the Equal Protection Clause when it subordinates all traditional, neutral districting principles to the overarching goal of drawing a congressional redistricting map that achieves maximum partisan advantage for members of one political party.
> 3. Whether a State exceeds its delegated power under Article I of the Constitution when it draws congressional-district boundaries to ensure that candidates from one political party will consistently capture a supermajority of the State's congressional seats even if those candidates win less than half the popular vote statewide.

### OT2004: Johnson v. California, 543 U.S. 499 (No. 03-636)

Area: Race.
Status: Open. The common standard governs racial classifications generally; whether prison deference changes that is not decided.
> 1. Is a state's practice of routine racial segregation of state prisoners for at least a 60-day period subject to the same strict scrutiny generally applicable to all other challenges to intentional racial segregation, or is it excused from such scrutiny and subject only to the more relaxed review afforded under Turner v. Safley, 482 U.S. 78 (1987)?
> 2. Does California's practice of routine racial segregation of state prisoners for at least a 60-day period violate the Equal Protection Clause?

### OT2005: League of United Latin American Citizens v. Perry, 548 U.S. 399 (Nos. 05-204, 05-254, 05-276, 05-439)

Area: Representation; VRA §2; territorial districting.
Status: Open. No. 05-276 Question 3 assumes Bush v. Vera's historical racial-gerrymandering rule, so that question's predicate is removed here.
No. 05-204:
> 1. Whether the 2003 Texas Congressional Redistricting Plan (Plan 1374C), adopted and developed using out-dated, inaccurate 2000 Census data and resulting in malapportioned districts, in violation of one person, one vote when measured against 2003 Census data, and when "the single-minded purpose of the Texas Legislature in enacting Plan 1374C was to gain partisan advantage" and when such purpose is realized, is an unconstitutional political gerrymander.
> 2. Whether proof of racially polarized voting is overcome by evidence of partisan affiliation of minority voters in the analysis of the second prong of Gingles in a minority vote dilution claim.

No. 05-439 (GI Forum v. Perry):
> 1. Whether political partisanship is sufficient justification, under section 2 and the Constitution, for dismantling a Latino-majority congressional district in order to elect the Anglo-preferred candidate.
> 2. Whether section 2 permits a state to eliminate a majority-minority district located in one area of the state and create another majority-minority district in a different area of the state.
> 3. Whether the District Court erred by requiring section 2 demonstrative districts to be more compact and to offer greater electoral opportunity to minority voters than the corresponding districts in the challenged redistricting plan.
> 4. Whether the number of majority-minority districts that can be created in the state functions as the upper limit of permissible political opportunity when assessing proportionality under Johnson v. DeGrandy.

No. 05-276 (Jackson v. Perry):
> 1. Whether the Equal Protection Clause and the First Amendment prohibit States from redrawing lawful districting plans in the middle of the decade, for the sole purpose of maximizing partisan advantage.
> 2. Whether Section 2 of the Voting Rights Act permits a State to destroy a district effectively controlled by African-American voters, merely because it is impossible to draw a district in which African-Americans constitute an absolute mathematical majority of the population.
> 3. Whether, under Bush v. Vera, 517 U.S. 952 (1996), a bizarre-looking congressional district, which was intentionally drawn as a majority-Latino district by connecting two far-flung pockets of dense urban population with a 300-mile-long rural "land bridge," may escape invalidation as a racial gerrymander because drawing a compact majority-Latino district would have required the mapmakers to compromise their political goal of maximizing Republican seats elsewhere in the State.

No. 05-254 (Travis County v. Perry):
> Does the Texas legislature's 2003 replacement of a legally valid congressional districting plan with a state-wide plan, enacted for "the single-minded purpose" of gaining partisan advantage, satisfy the stringent constitutional rule of equipopulous districts by relying on the 2000 decennial census and the fiction of inter-censal population accuracy?

### OT2006: Parents Involved in Community Schools v. Seattle School District No. 1, 551 U.S. 701 (No. 05-908), with Meredith v. Jefferson County Board of Education (No. 05-915)

Area: Race (K-12).
Status: Partly decided. Eisenberg Pt II answered whether supported K-12 integration can be an important objective. Open: whether individual students may be assigned by race, and how. Brewer v. West Irondequoit is being deleted to keep that open.
No. 05-908:
> (1) How are the Equal Protection rights of public high school students affected by the jurisprudence of Grutter v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 306 (2003), and Gratz v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 244 (2003)?
> (2) Is racial diversity a compelling interest that can justify the use of race in selecting students for admission to public high schools?
> (3) May a school district that is not racially segregated and that normally permits a student to attend any high school of her choosing deny a child admission to her chosen school solely because of her race in an effort to achieve a desired racial balance in particular schools, or does such racial balancing violate the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment?

No. 05-915:
> 1. Should Grutter v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 306 (2003) and Regents of University of California v. Bakke, 438 U.S. 268 (1978) and Gratz v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 244 (2003) be overturned and/or misapplied by the Respondent, the Jefferson County Board of Education to use race as the sole factor to assign students to the regular (non-traditional) schools in the Jefferson County Public Schools?
> 2. Whether the race-conscious Student Assignment Plan with mechanical and inflexible quota systems of not less than 15% nor greater than 50% of African American students without individually or holistic review of any student, meets the Fourteenth Amendment requirement of the use of race which is a compelling interest narrowly tailored with strict scrutiny.
> 3. Did the District Court abuse and/or exceed its remedial judicial authority in maintaining desegregative attractiveness in the Public Schools of Jefferson County, Kentucky?

### OT2007: Crawford v. Marion County Election Board, 553 U.S. 181 (No. 07-21)

Area: Franchise and counting.
Status: Open. Will be framed by Stone's Bush v. Gore rule and Burdick.
> Whether an Indiana statute mandating that those seeking to vote in-person produce a government-issued photo identification violates the First and Fourteenth Amendments to the United States Constitution.

### OT2008: Ricci v. DeStefano, 557 U.S. 557 (No. 07-1428)

Area: Race (employment; statutory duties).
Status: Open.
[Preamble omitted.]
> 1. When an otherwise valid civil service selection process yields unintended racially disproportionate results, may municipalities reject the results and the successful candidates for reasons of race absent the demonstration required by 42 U.S.C. § 2000e-2(k)?
> 2. Does 42 U.S.C. §2000e-2(l) which makes it unlawful for employers "to adjust the scores of, use different cutoff scores for, or otherwise alter the results of, employment related tests on the basis of race ... ," permit employers to refuse to act on the results of such tests for reasons of race?
> 3. If, citing the public interest in eradicating political patronage, racism and corruption in civil service, a state's highest court mandates strict compliance with local laws requiring race-blind competitive merit selection procedures, does 42 U.S.C. §2000e-7 permit federal courts to relieve municipalities from compliance with such laws?

### OT2008: Bartlett v. Strickland, 556 U.S. 1 (No. 07-689)

Area: VRA §2.
Status: Pre-decided by Valdespino (OT1998).
> Whether a racial minority group that constitutes less than 50% of a proposed district's population can state a vote dilution claim under Section 2 of the Voting Rights Act, 42 U.S.C. § 1973.

### OT2008: Northwest Austin Municipal Utility District No. One v. Holder, 557 U.S. 193 (No. 08-322)

Area: VRA §5.
Status: Open.
> 1. Whether §4(a) of the Voting Rights Act, which permits "political subdivisions" of a State covered by §5's requirement that certain jurisdictions preclear changes affecting voting with the federal government to bail out of §5 coverage if they can establish a ten-year history of compliance with the VRA, must be available to any political subunit of a covered State when the Court's precedent requires "political subdivision" to be given its ordinary meaning throughout most of the VRA and no statutory text abrogates that interpretation with respect to §4(a).
> 2. Whether, under the Court's consistent jurisprudence requiring that remedial legislation be congruent and proportional to substantive constitutional guarantees, the 2006 enactment of the §5 preclearance requirement can be applied as a valid exercise of Congress's remedial powers under the Reconstruction Amendments when that enactment was founded on a congressional record demonstrating no evidence of a persisting pattern of attempts to evade court enforcement of voting-rights guarantees in jurisdictions covered only on the basis of data 35 or more years old, or even when considered under a purportedly less stringent rational-basis standard

### OT2012: Shelby County v. Holder, 570 U.S. 529 (No. 12-96)

Area: VRA §5.
Status: Open.
Question as granted:
> Whether Congress' decision in 2006 to reauthorize Section 5 of the Voting Rights Act under the pre-existing coverage formula of Section 4(b) of the Voting Rights Act exceeded its authority under the Fourteenth and Fifteenth Amendments and thus violated the Tenth Amendment and Article IV of the United States Constitution.

### OT2012: Fisher v. University of Texas at Austin, 570 U.S. 297 (No. 11-345)

Area: Race (admissions).
Status: Partly decided, as Grutter.
> Whether this Court's decisions interpreting the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment, including Grutter v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 306 (2003), permit the University of Texas at Austin's use of race in undergraduate admissions decisions.

### OT2012: United States v. Windsor, 570 U.S. 744 (No. 12-307)

Area: Sexual orientation.
Status: Open.
[Preamble on DOMA §3 omitted.]
> Whether Section 3 of DOMA violates the Fifth Amendment's guarantee of equal protection of the laws as applied to persons of the same sex who are legally married under the laws of their State.

The Court added: whether the Executive Branch's agreement with the court below deprives the Court of jurisdiction, and whether the Bipartisan Legal Advisory Group has Article III standing.

### OT2013: Schuette v. Coalition to Defend Affirmative Action, 572 U.S. 291 (No. 12-682)

Area: Race (political process).
Status: Partly decided. Coalition for Economic Equity v. Wilson (OT1998) applied the political-process inquiry to Proposition 209.
> Whether a state violates the Equal Protection Clause by amending its constitution to prohibit race and sex-based discrimination or preferential treatment in public-university admissions decisions.

### OT2014: Alabama Legislative Black Caucus v. Alabama, 575 U.S. 254 (No. 13-895)

Area: Territorial districting.
Status: Changed. Its granted question is framed as intentional packing, so it survives here as an intentional-dilution claim (Section 3.1) rather than a predominance claim.
Question as granted (Question 2 of No. 13-895):
> 2. Whether Alabama's legislative redistricting plans unconstitutionally classify black voters by race by intentionally packing them in districts designed to maintain supermajority percentages produced when 2010 census data are applied to the 2001 majority-black districts.

The consolidated No. 13-1138 was granted on its own Question 1, not reproduced here.

### OT2014: Obergefell v. Hodges, 576 U.S. 644 (Nos. 14-556, 14-562, 14-571, 14-574)

Area: Sexual orientation.
Status: Open.
Questions as granted:
> 1) Does the Fourteenth Amendment require a State to license a marriage between two people of the same sex?
> 2) Does the Fourteenth Amendment require a State to recognize a marriage between two people of the same sex when their marriage was lawfully licensed and performed out-of-state?

### OT2015: Evenwel v. Abbott, 578 U.S. 54 (No. 14-940)

Area: Representation (population equality).
Status: Pre-decided once Chen v. City of Houston (OT2000) is decided.
[Preamble omitted.]
> The question presented is whether the "one-person, one-vote" principle of the Fourteenth Amendment creates a judicially enforceable right ensuring that the districting process does not deny voters an equal vote.

### OT2015: Fisher v. University of Texas at Austin, 579 U.S. 365 (No. 14-981)

Area: Race (admissions).
Status: Partly decided, as Grutter.
> Whether the Fifth Circuit's re-endorsement of the University of Texas at Austin's use of racial preferences in undergraduate admissions decisions can be sustained under this Court's decisions interpreting the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment, including Fisher v. University of Texas at Austin, 133 S. Ct. 2411 (2013).

### OT2016: Bethune-Hill v. Virginia State Board of Elections, 580 U.S. 178 (No. 15-680)

Area: Territorial districting.
Status: Predicate removed. Every question assumes racial predominance triggers strict scrutiny. Its 55% floor facts could return as an intentional-dilution or packing claim.
> 1. Did the court below err in holding that race cannot predominate even where it is the most important consideration in drawing a given district unless the use of race results in "actual conflict" with traditional districting criteria?
> 2. Did the court below err by concluding that the admitted use of a one-size-fits-all 55% black voting age population floor to draw twelve separate House of Delegates districts does not amount to racial predominance and trigger strict scrutiny?
> 3. Did the court below err in disregarding the admitted use of race in drawing district lines in favor of examining circumstantial evidence regarding the contours of the districts?
> 4. Did the court below err in holding that racial goals must negate all other districting criteria in order for race to predominate?
> 5. Did the court below err in concluding that the General Assembly's predominant use of race in drawing House District 75 was narrowly tailored to serve a compelling government interest?

### OT2016: Cooper v. Harris, 581 U.S. 285 (No. 15-1262, captioned McCrory v. Harris)

Area: Territorial districting.
Status: Predicate removed, as Bethune-Hill. Its Questions 1 and 2 also rely on Bartlett v. Strickland's majority rule, which Valdespino displaced.
[Preamble omitted.]
> 1. Did the court below err in presuming racial predominance from North Carolina's reasonable reliance on this Court's holding in Strickland that a district created to ensure that African Americans have an equal opportunity to elect their preferred candidate of choice complies with the VRA if it contains a numerical majority of African Americans?
> 2. Did the court below err in applying a standard of review that required the State to demonstrate its construction of CD 1 was "actually necessary" under the VRA instead of simply showing it had "good reasons" to believe the district, as created, was needed to foreclose future vote dilution claims?
> 3. Did the court below err in relieving plaintiffs of their burden to prove "race rather than politics" predominated with proof of an alternative plan that achieves the legislature's political goals, is comparably consistent with traditional redistricting principles, and brings about greater racial balance than the challenged districts?
> 4. Regardless of any other error, was the three-judge court's finding of racial gerrymandering violations based on clearly erroneous fact-finding?
> 5. Did the court below err in failing to dismiss plaintiffs' claims as being barred by claim preclusion or issue preclusion?
> 6. In the interests of judicial comity and federalism, should the Court order full briefing and oral argument to resolve the split between the court below and the North Carolina Supreme Court which reached the opposite result in a case raising identical claims?

### OT2016: Sessions v. Morales-Santana, 582 U.S. 47 (No. 15-1191)

Area: Sex.
Status: Pre-decided through Nguyen (OT2000), including the remedy.
[Preamble omitted.]
> 1. Whether Congress's decision to impose a different physical-presence requirement on unwed citizen mothers of foreign-born children than on other citizen parents of foreign-born children through 8 U.S.C. 1401 and 1409 (1958) violates the Fifth Amendment's guarantee of equal protection.
> 2. Whether the court of appeals erred in conferring U.S. citizenship on respondent, in the absence of any express statutory authority to do so.

### OT2017: Abbott v. Perez, 585 U.S. 579 (No. 17-586)

Area: Territorial districting; VRA §2.
Status: Open. Question 4 (racial gerrymandering) has its predicate removed; Questions 2 and 3 (unlawful purpose and intentional dilution) survive and depend on Section 3.1's elements.
> 1. Whether the district court issued an appealable interlocutory injunction when it invalidated Texas' duly enacted redistricting plan and ordered the parties to appear at a remedial hearing to redraw state congressional districts unless the Governor called a special legislative session to redraw the congressional map within three days.
> 2. Whether the Texas Legislature acted with an unlawful purpose when it enacted a redistricting plan originally imposed by the district court to remedy any potential constitutional and statutory defects in a prior legislative plan that was repealed without ever having taken effect.
> 3. Whether the Texas Legislature engaged in intentional vote dilution when it adopted Congressional District 27 in 2013 after the district court found, in 2012, that CD27 did not support a plausible claim of racially discriminatory purpose and did not dilute Hispanic voting strength because it was not possible to create an additional Hispanic opportunity district in the region;
> 4. Whether the Legislature engaged in racial gerrymandering in Congressional District 35 when it simply adopted the district unchanged as part of the court-ordered remedial plan.

### OT2018: Rucho v. Common Cause, 588 U.S. 684 (No. 18-422)

Area: Representation (partisan districting).
Status: Open.
[Preamble omitted.]
> 1. Whether plaintiffs have standing to press their partisan gerrymandering claims.
> 2. Whether plaintiffs' partisan gerrymandering claims are justiciable.
> 3. Whether North Carolina's 2016 congressional map is, in fact, an unconstitutional partisan gerrymander.

### OT2019: Bostock v. Clayton County, 590 U.S. 644 (No. 17-1618)

Area: Sexual orientation (Title VII).
Status: Open.
> Whether discrimination against an employee because of sexual orientation constitutes prohibited employment discrimination "because of... sex" within the meaning of Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964, 42 U.S.C. § 2000e-2.

### OT2020: Brnovich v. Democratic National Committee, 594 U.S. 647 (No. 19-1257)

Area: VRA §2 (vote denial); Fifteenth Amendment.
Status: Open.
[Preamble omitted.]
> 1. Does Arizona's out-of-precinct policy violate Section 2 of the Voting Rights Act?
> 2. Does Arizona's ballot-collection law violate Section 2 of the Voting Rights Act or the Fifteenth Amendment?

### OT2022: Allen v. Milligan, 599 U.S. 1 (No. 21-1086, captioned Merrill v. Milligan)

Area: VRA §2.
Status: Open on Gingles's application. The historical constitutional objection (that §2 compels race-based districting) is largely answered by Miller's no-injury rule.
Question as amended:
> Whether the State of Alabama's 2021 redistricting plan for its seven seats in the United States House of Representatives violated Section 2 of the Voting Rights Act, 52 U.S.C. §10301.

### OT2022: Students for Fair Admissions v. President and Fellows of Harvard College, 600 U.S. 181 (No. 20-1199), and v. University of North Carolina (No. 21-707)

Area: Race (admissions).
Status: Partly decided, as Grutter.
No. 20-1199:
> 1. Should this Court overrule Grutter v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 306 (2003), and hold that institutions of higher education cannot use race as a factor in admissions?
> 2. Title VI of the Civil Rights Act bans race-based admissions that, if done by a public university, would violate the Equal Protection Clause. Gratz v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 244, 276 n.23 (2003). Is Harvard violating Title VI by penalizing Asian-American applicants, engaging in racial balancing, overemphasizing race, and rejecting workable race-neutral alternatives?

No. 21-707:
> 1. Should this Court overrule Grutter v. Bollinger, 539 U.S. 306 (2003), and hold that institutions of higher education cannot use race as a factor in admissions?
> 2. Can a university reject a race-neutral alternative because it would change the composition of the student body, without proving that the alternative would cause a dramatic sacrifice in academic quality or the educational benefits of overall student-body diversity?

### OT2022: Moore v. Harper, 600 U.S. 1 (No. 21-1271)

Area: Franchise and counting (Elections Clause).
Status: Open. Will be framed by Stone's Article II answer in Bush v. Gore.
> Whether a State's judicial branch may nullify the regulations governing the "Manner of holding Elections for Senators and Representatives . . . prescribed . . . by the Legislature thereof," U.S. CONST. art. I, § 4, cl. 1, and replace them with regulations of the state courts' own devising, based on vague state constitutional provisions purportedly vesting the state judiciary with power to prescribe whatever rules it deems appropriate to ensure a "fair" or "free" election.

### OT2023: Alexander v. South Carolina State Conference of the NAACP, 602 U.S. 1 (No. 22-807)

Area: Territorial districting.
Status: Mostly predicate removed (Questions 1 to 5 concern racial predominance). Question 6, an intentional-discrimination claim without proof of discriminatory effect, survives and depends on Section 3.1's dilution elements.
[Preamble omitted.]
> 1. Did the district court err when it failed to apply the presumption of good faith and to holistically analyze District 1 and the General Assembly's intent?
