> 2. Did the district court err in failing to enforce the alternative-map requirement in this circumstantial case?
> 3. Did the district court err when it failed to disentangle race from politics?
> 4. Did the district court err in finding racial predominance when it never analyzed District 1's compliance with traditional districting principles?
> 5. Did the district court clearly err in finding that the General Assembly used a racial target as a proxy for politics when the record showed only that the General Assembly was aware of race, that race and politics are highly correlated, and that the General Assembly drew districts based on election data?
> 6. Did the district court err in upholding the intentional discrimination claim when it never even considered whether-let alone found that-District 1 has a discriminatory effect?

### OT2024: United States v. Skrmetti, 605 U.S. ___ (No. 23-477)

Area: Gender identity; sex.
Status: Open.
> Whether Tennessee Senate Bill 1 (SB1), which prohibits all medical treatments intended to allow "a minor to identify with, or live as, a purported identity inconsistent with the minor's sex" or to treat "purported discomfort or distress from a discordance between the minor's sex and asserted identity," Tenn. Code Ann. § 68-33-103(a)(1), violates the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment.

### OT2024 to OT2025: Louisiana v. Callais (Nos. 24-109, 24-110)

Area: Territorial districting; VRA §2.
Status: Mostly pre-decided. Questions 1 and 2 assume predominance triggers strict scrutiny, which this timeline rejects; the supplemental question is largely answered by Miller Pt IV (absence of federal compulsion does not itself prohibit voluntary inclusion). Restored for reargument; the historical decision is not reflected here.
[Preamble omitted.]
> 1. Did the majority err in finding that race predominated in the Legislature's enactment of S.B. 8?
> 2. Did the majority err in finding that S.B. 8 fails strict scrutiny?
> 3. Did the majority err in subjecting S.B. 8 to the Gingles preconditions?
> 4. Is this action non-justiciable?

Supplemental question (order of August 1, 2025):
> Whether the State's intentional creation of a second majority-minority congressional district violates the Fourteenth or Fifteenth Amendments to the U.S. Constitution.

### OT2025: Little v. Hecox (No. 24-38) and West Virginia v. B.P.J. (No. 24-43)

Area: Sex; gender identity.
Status: Open. Sex-equality questions may be closed early; whether the sex-separated-teams question counts as sex equality or stays reserved with gender identity is still to be decided.
No. 24-38 [preamble omitted]:
> Whether laws that seek to protect women's and girls' sports by limiting participation to women and girls based on sex violate the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment.

No. 24-43 [preamble omitted]:

> 2. Whether the Equal Protection Clause prevents a state from offering separate boys' and girls' sports teams based on biological sex determined at birth.

## 3. The doctrine as it stands

Each area states the rule as of June 29, 2000, how a court applies it step by step, and which landmarks are waiting on it.

### 3.1 Territorial districting

Doctrine now: deliberate or predominant use of race in drawing a shared territorial district is not, by itself, an equal-protection injury, a trigger for strict scrutiny, or a basis for an injunction (Shaw, Miller, Vera, Hunt). A State may consider racial conditions to improve electoral opportunity even where federal law does not require the district (Miller Pt IV, nine votes on that limited point). Racial exclusion, intentional dilution, materially unequal participation and legally inferior civic status remain actionable under their own elements, and a status or participation injury can be complete when government imposes it, without electoral or financial loss (Miller Pt III). Voters personally subjected to the challenged assignment have standing; mere residence elsewhere is not enough (Miller Pt I-A, Vera). When motive is material to a cognizable claim, a genuine race-versus-politics dispute goes to trial (Hunt Pt I).

How it operates:

1. Identify the claimant's specific injury. If the only complaint is the district's racial composition, the claim fails on the merits.
2. Confirm standing: personal exposure to the challenged assignment.
3. Apply the elements of the injury claimed. These have not been defined; Section 4.1 defines them.
4. Shape, demographics and statements are evidence for a cognizable claim, never a substitute for one.
5. Relief follows the injury proved; no redrawing for racial balance.

Reserved for: Easley (OT2000); LULAC (OT2005); Alabama Legislative Black Caucus (OT2014, as intentional packing); Abbott v. Perez (OT2017); Alexander (OT2023, Question 6); Callais (OT2025).

### 3.2 VRA §2

Doctrine now: §2 is a results statute; intent is not required. The Gingles conditions are necessary but not sufficient, followed by the totality of circumstances (De Grandy). Proportionality is evidence, not a safe harbor, and there is no duty to maximize. The first Gingles condition can be met by a majority district or by rigorous proof that a lawful, compact district would let a cohesive group with dependable crossover support elect its candidates (Valdespino). A challenge to governing-body size needs a reasonable, workable comparator rooted in law and practice (Holder). Judicial elections are covered, with state linkage interests weighed (SCLC). §2 reaches the direct replacement of an existing election by appointment (Mixon). A State need not prove remedial necessity before drawing majority-minority districts (Voinovich), and federal courts defer to timely state redistricting (Growe).

How it operates:

1. Gingles 1: a majority district, or a functional crossover district proved rigorously.
2. Gingles 2 and 3: cohesion and bloc voting that usually defeats the group's candidates.
3. Totality of circumstances, with proportionality as one factor.
4. A remedy that is a lawful plan, with the State given the first chance to draw it.

Reserved for: LULAC (OT2005); Bartlett (OT2008, pre-decided); Brnovich (OT2020, vote denial); Allen v. Milligan (OT2022).

### 3.3 VRA §5

Doctrine now: covered jurisdictions must preclear changes, including changes a noncovered State compels (Lopez I and II), discretionary changes made in implementing the NVRA (Young), county poll-official selection methods (Foreman), and a party convention fee (Morse, with a private §10 action). The effect test is retrogression (Bossier I). The purpose test bars a dilutive purpose even without intended retrogression (Bossier I Pt III; Bossier II). A §2 violation is not an independent bar to clearance. Reallocation of governing authority with no direct relation to voting is not covered (Presley).

How it operates:

1. Is the jurisdiction covered, and is this a change affecting voting?
2. Effect: does the change make the group worse off than the benchmark?
3. Purpose: was it adopted with a dilutive purpose, whether or not retrogression was intended?
4. Without preclearance, the change cannot be implemented, and courts may not give it a route around §5.

Reserved for: Georgia v. Ashcroft (OT2002); NAMUDNO (OT2008); Shelby County (OT2012).

### 3.4 Franchise, counting and representation

Doctrine now: ancestry cannot define the electorate for a public state office (Rice, Fifteenth Amendment). Election regulations are reviewed under the calibrated burden framework (Burdick, Timmons, Norman). The census may count federal personnel overseas who have enduring residence ties (Franklin); the Census Act and the Enumeration Clause permit the sampling methods at issue in Commerce v. House. The Elections Clause supports the NVRA's registration duties (Voting Rights Coalition). States may not add congressional qualifications through ballot rules (Term Limits). No controlling law yet addresses equal treatment of ballots in recounts, or Article II and state-court construction of election law; Bush v. Gore is first impression.

How it operates:

1. Identify the interest burdened and the severity of the burden.
2. Severe or discriminatory burdens need strong justification; reasonable nondiscriminatory rules get latitude.
3. Racial denial of the franchise is categorically barred, not balanced.

Reserved for: Bush v. Palm Beach and Bush v. Gore (OT2000); Vieth (OT2003); Crawford (OT2007); Evenwel (OT2015, pre-decided through Chen); Rucho (OT2018); Moore v. Harper (OT2022).

### 3.5 Governmental racial classifications

Doctrine now: Adarand's common substantial-justification standard governs federal, state and local government. Government must show an important objective within its lawful authority, a substantial, evidence-supported relationship between the racial treatment and that objective, and justification of the measure's coverage and burdens, including a response to concrete, practicable alternatives. Supported correction, prevention of recurrence, and overcoming barriers to market participation are permissible objectives. Racial hierarchy, stereotype, hostility, political favoritism and racial balancing for its own sake are not. It has been applied to admissions (Hopwood: separate tracks fail; supported diversity can qualify, six votes), contracting (Engineering Contractors: race-neutral alternatives must be seriously considered), promotions (Dallas: no connected justification and no fit), and K-12 integration (Eisenberg: a supported integration objective can qualify, six votes). A selective transfer of racial-remedy authority to a more remote political process triggers the political-process inquiry (Coalition v. Wilson). Unequal opportunity to compete is itself an injury, though proof of the same decision defeats a claim to the seat or compensation (Lesage). Desegregation remedies track the adjudicated violation, and release requires good-faith compliance and elimination of vestiges to the extent practicable (Freeman, Jenkins, Fordice).

How it operates:

1. Is there actual differential racial treatment of the claimant?
2. Government names its objective. Is it within its lawful authority and permissible?
3. Does evidence connect the racial treatment to that objective substantially?
4. Are the coverage, the burden on others, and the duration justified, with a real answer to practical alternatives?
5. If any step fails, the provision or application fails, and the remedy is confined to it.

Reserved for: Grutter and Gratz (OT2002); Johnson v. California (OT2004); Parents Involved (OT2006, method only); Ricci (OT2008); Fisher I (OT2012); Schuette (OT2013); Fisher II (OT2015); SFFA (OT2022).

### 3.6 Sex

Doctrine now: government must show an actual important objective and a substantial relationship. "Exceedingly persuasive justification" describes that burden; it is not strict scrutiny (VMI, J.E.B., Kelley, Klinger, Cohen, Nabozny, Dallas Pt I). A separate program must give substantially equal opportunity (VMI). Comparison is program-specific (Klinger). Sex-conscious restructuring needs an actual objective, not ratios or budgets alone (Kelley). A sex-conscious remedy needs its own sex-specific record (Dallas; Engineering Contractors' women's program failed). Sex-based citizenship-transmission rules have no controlling rule; Miller v. Albright fractured.

How it operates:

1. Is there a sex classification, express or proven by purpose?
2. Government shows the actual objective and why it is important.
3. Government shows the classification substantially serves it, without stereotypes or overbroad generalizations.

Reserved for: Nguyen (OT2000, being decided); Morales-Santana (OT2016, pre-decided through Nguyen); Skrmetti (OT2024) and Hecox and B.P.J. (OT2025), as far as they are sex questions.

### 3.7 Sexual orientation and gender identity

Doctrine now: orientation classifications receive ordinary rational-basis review tied to the actual disadvantage; no heightened tier (Romer, Equality Foundation, Nabozny, Stemler, Shahar Pt III). The military discharge cases sustained genuinely rebuttable conduct-related inferences (Walmer, Thomasson, Richenberg, each 5-4). Stone's intermediate-scrutiny view is a stated minority position (Walmer, Shahar). Gender identity has no case; Kelley expressly left it open.

How it operates:

1. Identify the actual disadvantage imposed.
2. Is there a legitimate purpose rationally related to that disadvantage? Animus and bare hostility fail (Romer).

Reserved for: Lawrence (OT2002); Windsor (OT2012); Obergefell (OT2014); Bostock (OT2019); Skrmetti (OT2024); Hecox and B.P.J. (OT2025).

### 3.8 Other classifications

Doctrine now: disability receives rational basis tied to the actual disadvantage, with heightened scrutiny not reached (Heller v. Doe). Economic classifications need any reasonably conceivable basis, which the challenger must negate (Beach, Nordlinger, Burlington Northern). A class-of-one claim needs intentional differential treatment without a rational basis; ill will is not an element (Olech). New residents receive equal treatment under the Citizenship and Privileges or Immunities Clauses, and Congress cannot authorize otherwise (Saenz). Wealth is not a suspect class, but the first appeal from a permanent parental termination cannot be conditioned on payment (M.L.B.). Discovery on a racial selective-prosecution claim requires some credible evidence of discriminatory effect and purpose, including a reliable basis to infer that similarly situated persons of another race could have been prosecuted but were not; less than a prima facie case, with no fixed checklist (Armstrong). Jury strikes may not rest on race or sex (McCollum, J.E.B.), and a white defendant may challenge discrimination against Black grand jurors (Campbell).

Reserved for: no listed landmark through OT2025.

### 3.9 Independent civic injuries: questions to carry into the cases

The completed *Shaw–Miller–Vera–Hunt* line rejects racial district design as a sufficient constitutional injury and preserves independent exclusion, dilution, unequal participation, and civic-status claims. This subsection tracks the questions those reservations leave. The complete proposed elements, definitions, evidentiary rules, defenses, remedies, and illustrative counts appear together in [section 6.1](#complete-test). The case-specific recommendations appear in [section 6.2](#complete-docket). Neither collection becomes a holding simply by being included in this tracker.

The former candidate outline is superseded as a drafting plan by that complete text. In particular, it must not continue to suggest that only dilution can justify changing boundaries, that only the State's criteria can ground a comparison, or that every participation injury requires loss of a winnable district. Which rule the Court has actually adopted remains a separate, dated question.

#### 3.9.1 Racial exclusion

What government act removes eligibility to vote, have a ballot counted, or belong to the political unit? Was race an operative criterion, including ancestry used as race? Does a candidacy restriction require an express extension beyond the franchise holding? What relief restores the affected person's eligibility or membership? *Gomillion*, the white-primary cases, *Hunter v. Underwood*, and the simulated *Rice* identify the distinct mechanisms to examine.

#### 3.9.2 Intentional vote dilution

What effective electoral opportunity was materially impaired, compared with which lawful and workable alternative? Does the evidence show action because of that impairment, rather than racial awareness alone? Can packing be distinguished from a justified margin supporting a real opportunity district? How do *White v. Regester*, *Rogers v. Lodge*, *Holder*, and the simulated crossover cases inform the proof without turning the constitutional claim into §2 or a right to influence, a racial percentage, or proportional seats?

#### 3.9.3 Materially unequal participation

What registration, voting, counting, organizing, coalition-building, or constituent-access activity does the government obstruct? Is there actual association or realistic participation to protect, including concrete efforts to begin it? What specific mechanism imposes materially worse terms, and how is race connected to those terms? Can that injury arise without electoral defeat? If a boundary causes the obstruction, what boundary or administrative correction would remove it? *Lane v. Wilson*, *McLaurin*, and the fictional *Miller* reservation supply comparisons; they do not eliminate the need to identify the actual injury.

#### 3.9.4 Legally inferior civic status

Does an official rule, ballot, record, or qualification give race legal operation in a person's civic status? How does an operative designation differ from a legislator's descriptive remark, which may instead be evidence of another claim? What follows from *Anderson v. Martin* when every race is labeled? Which election-administration data collections remain legitimate? What distinct legal source governs nonracial ballot labels such as those in *Cook*?

#### 3.9.5 Shared questions

For each operation: who is personally injured; what law protects the interest; what must be pleaded and proved; who bears each burden; what lawful comparison answers the causal question; what defense is available; and what authorized remedy removes the proved injury? Which matters are jurisdictional standing questions, and which belong to the merits?

#### 3.9.6 Questions reserved for particular disputes

- *Georgia v. Ashcroft*: when does a changed district preserve effective opportunity, and when does an asserted exchange for influence amount to retrogression?
- *LULAC*: what follows from a mid-decade replacement, a claimed geographic offset, and overlapping statutory, racial-purpose, and partisan claims?
- *Abbott v. Perez*: how do the operative enactment, proof of purpose, and a claimed presumption of good faith interact?
- *Vieth* and *Rucho*: what judicially manageable partisan injury, lawful comparison, and remedy can be defended without an entitlement to favorable seats?
- *Easley* and *Alexander*: how can a court distinguish race from party where purpose is material, while refusing to treat partisanship as immunity?

#### 3.9.7 OT2000 threshold questions

- *Sinkfield*: what injury do these plaintiffs themselves suffer, and how should the standing statements in *Miller* and Stone's *Vera* writing be reconciled?
- *Easley*: what independent claim survived the *Hunt* mandate, if any, and what historical posture must change in this timeline?
- *Chen*: what population measure governs representation, and is any independent racial deprivation actually presented?

The older proposed answers are preserved in [section 6.3](#carried-forward-plans), with their status and unresolved tensions stated there.

#### 3.9.8 What an opinion must resolve expressly

Is the proposed rule an application, clarification, extension, or change of precedent? What legal source supports it? Which elements, burdens, comparisons, and defenses actually matter to the presented claim? Does an unnecessary-packing inference remain circumstantial rather than automatic? Must racial assignment to worse participation terms be separately proved purposeful? What makes an official designation operative? What does the judgment leave open, and what exactly may the lower court do on remand?

#### 3.9.9 Vehicle questions and term placement

| Vehicle | Question it can present | Placement in the complete plan |
|---|---|---|
| *Sinkfield v. Kelley* | Personal injury and the relation between standing and the asserted deprivation | OT2000 carried-forward material |
| *Easley v. Cromartie* | Preserved independent injury, mandate, and the role of official statements | OT2000 carried-forward material |
| *Black v. McGuffage*, 209 F. Supp. 2d 889 (2002) | Unequal ballot loss, statutory access, and the distinction between racial disparity and constitutional racial treatment | OT2001 lower-court comparison; no invented Supreme Court grant |
| *Cano v. Davis*, 211 F. Supp. 2d 1208 (2002) | Purpose, effective electoral opportunity, and a distinct participation or coalition injury | OT2002; claim-by-claim appellate preservation must be checked |
| *Arakaki v. Hawaii*, 314 F.3d 1091 (2002) | Ancestry-based candidacy restrictions and the proper constitutional source | Proposed OT2003 review; the lower decision remains dated December 31, 2002 |
| *Spencer v. Blackwell*, 347 F. Supp. 2d 528 (2004) | Polling-place challenges, state action, threatened unequal participation, and emergency relief | OT2004 emergency vehicle |
| *Johnson v. Governor of Florida*, 405 F.3d 1214 (2005) (en banc) | Discriminatory purpose and the effect of a later reenactment | Proposed OT2005 review |
| *Bone Shirt v. Hazeltine*, 461 F.3d 1011 (2006) | Packing, genuine opportunity, and a statutory remedy | Proposed OT2006 appellate vehicle; the 2004 district decision remains part of its history |

The term is the planned Supreme Court slot or expressly labeled comparison slot, not a rewriting of the lower court's decision date. The full docket explains each posture and identifies the actual evidence still needed. In *Cano*, the real court assumed challenged purpose evidence and ruled on insufficient effect; it did not make a dispositive finding that racial purpose was absent.

### 3.10 Where Stone's earlier materials still disagree

These are conflicts among Stone's earlier planned positions, his stated jurisprudence and his recorded votes. Each still needs a decision, because a position Stone states in a case that silently follows one source will contradict another. Earlier conflicts that are no longer open: Article I abrogation (decided: Stone keeps the position his votes made law) and the Dallas Fire Fighters disposition (the Court's decision fixes it).

1. Sexual-orientation tier. Stone's stated jurisprudence lists orientation as Elevated (intermediate scrutiny), and Stone's separate writings in Walmer and Shahar propose intermediate scrutiny as an openly stated change. But he wrote Nabozny for a unanimous Court applying rational basis to orientation, joined Romer and Equality Foundation on rational basis, and joined Stevens's rationality dissents in Thomasson and Richenberg. This is coherent only under a stated practice: apply governing law when writing for the Court, press intermediate scrutiny in separate writings. That practice is nowhere written down. See question O1.
2. Disability tier and the equal-protection modification duty. Stone's stated jurisprudence lists disability as Elevated and states an equal-protection duty of reasonable modification for government-controlled civic institutions. His earlier Garrett plan said Stone "does not make disability a suspect classification." Heller v. Doe, which Stone joined, applied rational basis and did not reach heightened scrutiny. See question O2.
3. Territorial standing. Stone wrote Miller Pt I-A (nine votes: voters personally subjected to the challenged assignment have standing), then in Vera proposed narrowing it so assignment alone does not supply injury. His earlier Vera plan and its corrections followed the Vera position. Stone's stated jurisprudence on elections says the territorial rule "is a position on the electoral claim, not a general restriction on standing," which matches Miller I-A, not his Vera writing. Should Stone abandon the Vera narrowing, and how would he reconcile personal exposure, the specific injury claimed and the distinction between standing and merits? See question D1.
4. The racial-justification structure. The Adarand holding is one common standard: important objective, substantial evidence-supported relationship, justification of coverage and burdens, and a response to concrete, practicable, comparably effective alternatives. Engineering Contractors Pt II (eight Associates) adds that race-neutral alternatives must be seriously considered. Stone's stated principles on equal political membership, impartial government and repair set out a two-route structure: public justification for prospective objectives, and a defined corrective permission whose satisfaction "resolves the corrective justification" so that a court does not "reopen the same justification through an additional impartiality balance." Their corrective conditions list no alternatives element, and Stone's Engineering Contractors dissent lists corrective requirements without one either. Either those principles restate Adarand, with the alternatives inquiry inside "reasonably fitted" (the candidate answer), or they propose a change to his own majority opinion, which his stated principle of candid change in precedent would require him to say. See question R1.
5. Remedy in sex-based citizenship cases. The earlier Nguyen plan and Stone's recorded Miller v. Albright dissent point toward different remedial approaches. The actual dissent favored a remand for a lawful remedy with three possibilities: an equal proof regime, an opportunity for Congress, or recognition of citizenship if no narrower cure exists. Which remedy would the statute and severability analysis authorize in Nguyen, and would Stone need to explain a change from that dissent? The tracker assigns the remedy question to Nguyen rather than postponing it to Morales-Santana. See question S2; the older recommendation is preserved in section 6.
6. Title VI private enforcement. Stone joined Powell's holding that the §602 disparate-impact regulation supports no implied action and no §1983 right. Can Sandoval be distinguished on a legally sufficient basis, or would the private-enforcement approach in the older plan require an express change to a precedent Stone joined? The earlier claim of consistency between the two needs justification rather than repetition. See question P1; the older recommendation is preserved in section 6.
7. Bush v. Gore counting standards. Stone's stated jurisprudence on elections requires counting standards "established by law or valid procedure, communicated before the count, and applied consistently." Can a statewide operational standard adopted after Election Day lawfully clarify the pre-election voter-intent rule for recurring ballot conditions? What makes that an implementation of existing law rather than a replacement, and does it satisfy the stated requirement that the standard be fixed in advance? See question F1; the older remedial recommendation is preserved in section 6.

## 4. Questions to answer before the landmarks

Each question has a vehicle, the landmark it must arrive before, and a blank answer field. Every vehicle not already on a case list needs an entry with its question presented and, where this timeline changes its posture, a reconstructed posture.

Branch policy. The landmark historical cases keep their central question open so this Court can decide them in their own cases. Lower-court vehicles are used to close the side questions that Stone's own doctrinal changes opened, so the landmarks arrive on a clean, coherent record. Cases should not decide more than their question requires; Vera decided more than it had to, which created branches that now need tracking word by word. Added November 6, 2026: collisions may be answered now and reinforced later, except where Parents Involved (OT2006) or the Obergefell line would be affected (section 5(b)). For Article I and Fourteenth Amendment §5 issues, Foundation 16 governs the use of separate or independently sufficient combined grounds; a planned later landmark does not require postponing a material question properly presented now. Questions carry one of four tags:

- Cleanup: a side question this Court's own changes opened, which no landmark will answer in the form this timeline needs. Close it with a vehicle before the related landmark arrives.
- Reserved: the central question of a landmark case. Leave it open. A vehicle that touches it decides on a narrower ground and names the question in its "Limits and questions not reached."
- Collision: a scheduled vehicle whose question presented is a landmark's central question. Decide whether to narrow the vehicle, accept that it pre-decides the landmark, or drop it.
- Pre-decided: a landmark whose central question an earlier decision of this Court has already answered. Note it, so the case list treats the landmark as changed, reconstructed or deleted under the causal rule.

Tracking: when a cleanup decision stops at the edge of a reserved question, the decision should name that question in its "Limits and questions not reached," so the reservation carries forward and it stays clear whether anything has closed it.

Already answered; do not reopen:

- Does the first Gingles condition require a 50% district? No; a cohesive group that can elect with dependable crossover support qualifies on rigorous proof (Valdespino, June 17, 1999, OT1998).
- Is predominant racial design of a district an injury by itself? No; it is not an injury, a scrutiny trigger, or a basis for an injunction (Shaw, Miller, Vera, Hunt).
- What standard governs racial classifications? The common substantial-justification standard (Adarand; Hopwood, Engineering Contractors, Dallas, Eisenberg).
- May a school district pursue K-12 integration without a desegregation decree? Yes, if supported, under the common standard (Eisenberg Pt II).
- Does VRA §5 reach dilutive purpose without intended backsliding? Yes (Bossier I Pt III, Bossier II).

### 4.1 Territorial districting

D1. Does standing follow the specific injury claimed, so that voters next to a challenged district may sue only for an injury of their own (for example dilution of their votes), and does Stone drop his Vera narrowing of Miller I-A?
> Answer before: OT2002, Cano v. Davis.

Vehicle: Sinkfield v. Kelley (OT2000, on the list).
Character answer:

D2. Is "other independently cognizable deprivation" a residual category, or a reference to deprivations recognized by their own precedents? Are legislators' official statements describing a district in racial terms a civic-status injury, or only evidence of purpose?
> Answer before: OT2002, Arakaki v. Hawaii.

Vehicle: Easley v. Cromartie (OT2000, on the list).
Character answer:

D3. Intentional dilution, purpose: does a legislature act with the required purpose when it limits the number of Latino voters in a district to protect an incumbent from a Latino-preferred challenger, without hostility toward Latino voters?
> Answer before: OT2005, League of United Latin American Citizens v. Perry; OT2017, Abbott v. Perez; OT2023, Alexander v. South Carolina State Conference of the NAACP.

Vehicle: Cano v. Davis, 211 F. Supp. 2d 1208 (C.D. Cal. 2002) (three-judge court), summarily affirmed, 537 U.S. 1100 (2003); slot OT2002.
Character answer:

D4. Intentional dilution, effect: once purpose is shown, what dilutive effect must be proved (the full Gingles showing, a relaxed version, or a reduced opportunity compared with a lawful alternative)?
> Answer before: OT2017, Abbott v. Perez; OT2023, Alexander v. South Carolina State Conference of the NAACP (Question 6).

Vehicle: Cano v. Davis (same). The district court called this standard "uncertain, largely because of a dearth of precedent."
Character answer:

D5. Packing: does concentrating a cohesive group far beyond what it needs to elect, when a lawful alternative would create another opportunity district, establish dilution, and is unnecessary packing strong evidence of purpose or only one factor?
> Answer before: OT2005, League of United Latin American Citizens v. Perry; OT2014, Alabama Legislative Black Caucus v. Alabama.

Vehicle: Bone Shirt v. Hazeltine, 336 F. Supp. 2d 976 (D.S.D. 2004), affirmed, 461 F.3d 1011 (8th Cir. 2006); slot OT2004.
Character answer:

D6. Unequal participation, terms and causation: is voting equipment that leaves materially more ballots uncounted where Black and Latino voters are concentrated (a) a nonracial unequal-treatment claim and (b) a racial claim, where the disparity correlates with race but no purpose is alleged?
> Answer before: OT2007, Crawford v. Marion County Election Board; OT2020, Brnovich v. Democratic National Committee.

Vehicle: Black v. McGuffage, 209 F. Supp. 2d 889 (N.D. Ill. 2002); interlocutory posture, needs a certification premise; slot OT2002.
Character answer:

D7. Unequal participation, racial assignment: does a challenge system alleged to target Black voters impose unequal participation on account of race; is the injury complete before any voter is turned away; are party-designated challengers state actors?
> Answer before: OT2007, Crawford v. Marion County Election Board; OT2020, Brnovich v. Democratic National Committee.

Vehicle: Spencer v. Blackwell, 347 F. Supp. 2d 528 (S.D. Ohio 2004); stayed, 388 F.3d 547 (6th Cir. 2004); Spencer v. Pugh, 543 U.S. 1301 (2004) (Stevens, J., in chambers); slot OT2004. Precinct-level facts not yet confirmed.
Character answer:

### 4.6 Reserved for the landmarks, and questions the Court left open

Reserved questions (leave open, and a vehicle that touches one decides on a narrower ground and names it in "Limits and questions not reached"):

- Influence districts and retrogression under §5: Georgia v. Ashcroft (OT2002).
- The §2 vote-denial test (rules on how people vote, not district lines): Brnovich (OT2020). Vehicles in between should decide on narrower grounds.
- Separating race from party: Easley (OT2000) at trial level; Alexander (OT2023).
- Partisan entrenchment: Vieth (OT2003), LULAC (OT2005), Rucho (OT2018).
- VRA §5 coverage, bailout and the Fifteenth Amendment enforcement standard: NAMUDNO (OT2008), Shelby County (OT2012).
- Constitutionality of the §2 results test in redistricting: pre-decided in part, since Miller's no-injury rule answers most of Allen v. Milligan's (OT2022) historical objection; its Gingles-application question stays reserved.
- Higher-education diversity: Grutter and Gratz (OT2002), Fisher (OT2012, OT2015), SFFA (OT2022). Hopwood Pt II pre-decides only that a supported diversity interest can qualify; method (points or individual review), how much diversity, duration and deference stay open.
- K-12 assignment methods (whether individual students may be assigned by race): Parents Involved (OT2006). Eisenberg answered the objective.
- Discarding a test to avoid Title VII disparate-impact liability: Ricci (OT2008).
- Presumption of legislative good faith: Abbott v. Perez (OT2017).
- Sexual-orientation tier: Lawrence (OT2002), Windsor (OT2012), Obergefell (OT2014).
- Gender identity: Bostock (OT2019, Title VII), Skrmetti (OT2024).
- Disability questions not resolved by Garrett's Title I judgment, including Title II court access in Lane (OT2003); apply Foundation 16 when separate or combined grounds are properly presented and material.
- Remedy direction in sex-based citizenship cases (extend the better rule to the disfavored group, or apply the worse rule to everyone): historically Morales-Santana (OT2016); here Nguyen (OT2000) decides it, so Morales-Santana becomes an application.

Questions the Court expressly reserved, as of June 29, 2000:

- Equality: Adarand's §8(d) regulatory conflict; Hopwood's federal classifications and employment; Eisenberg's policy validity and Kennedy's reservation; Engineering Contractors' sufficiency of the racial evidence and a redesigned women's program; Dallas's full plan, future promotions and Scalia's reservation on repair; J.E.B.'s private-party strikes; Virginia's private single-sex education and parallel programs; Kelley's orientation and gender identity; Romer's tier and Bowers; the military cases' §654(b)(1) and (b)(3), APA, procedural due process, liberty and speech claims; Heller's heightened scrutiny for disability; M.L.B.'s other civil litigation; Miller v. Albright's scrutiny level, remedy and the support and presence conditions; Campbell's fair-cross-section claim; Olech's ill-will theory; Saenz's broader Slaughter-House question; Lesage's §1981, §1983 and Title VI remedies; Powell's regulation validity and intent theories.
- Voting: influence and coalition claims; the §2 denominator; Fifteenth Amendment dilution; elements of independent districting injuries; VRA compliance as a compelling interest; nonresident standing; whether a districting decree needs an established violation (Lawyer); Hunt's state-law condition and the replacement map; the §5 same-decision defense; wholly federally compelled conduct (Young); elective-to-appointive replacement under §5; bailout and retrospective relief; Morse's refund, defendant and baseline questions; Rice's Fourteenth Amendment claim, tribal elections, benefits and candidate qualifications.
- Enforcement: Smith and the Fifteenth Amendment in Boerne; Morrison's color-of-law applications; catalyst fees. The state-immunity reservations are State amenability under IGRA, waiver by litigation conduct, Daniels for patents, other Lanham theories under §5, ADEA §5, IDEA abrogation and §504 waiver, whether immunity is jurisdictional and whether removal waives it, and ADA Title II abrogation.

## 5. Docket decisions and carried-forward plans

### (a) Post-Shaw docket instructions still in force

The earlier post-Shaw docket table has been carried out or overtaken. Hays, Shaw v. Hunt / Pope and Abrams were deleted. Miller (OT1994) and Vera (OT1995) were reconstructed as counterfactual grants and decided. The instruction to delete Hunt v. Cromartie was superseded when the Court decided Hunt in OT1998, and the instruction to delete Easley v. Cromartie was superseded by the OT2000 list, where Easley's posture must be reconstructed (question D13). What remains live:

1. The causal rule. A changed precedent removes a later historical case only when the later cause of action, standing theory, judgment, remedy or procedural posture materially depended on the rule that changed. A famous historical caption is not preserved merely because it would be useful. A reconstructed grant is used only when a genuine surviving legal question can be grounded in the historical dispute and is marked openly as counterfactual.
2. The independent voting-rights line stays on its own schedule. Later statutory or constitutional voting cases are not deleted merely because Shaw changed; their own statutes, constitutional claims, records and procedural histories control.
3. Each retained case supplies the historical issue, Stone's proposed disposition of the judgment that actually exists here, and his reasoning; reconstructed grants are expressly identified. A remand states what remains to be proved and does not manufacture favorable findings. A later case whose record an earlier judgment of this Court has altered must be revalidated before it is run.
4. A "vehicle" is an actual lower-court case this Court takes up. Its OT placement follows its assigned slot, while its actual reporter citation and decision year stay separately identified; this Court's grant of a vehicle is not a historical Supreme Court grant.
5. Stone can take these positions whether he writes for the Court, concurs or dissents; which description applies depends on the votes actually cast.

### (b) OT2000 collision decisions

Decided November 6, 2026: collisions may be answered now and reinforced later, except where Parents Involved (OT2006) or the Obergefell line would be affected.

- Daggett v. Commission on Governmental Ethics & Election Practices (No. 10, chunk 1). Collides with Arizona Free Enterprise (OT2010). Decision: delete. Note: only Question 2 (matching funds triggered by an opponent's spending) is Arizona Free Enterprise's question. Questions 1 (Maine's $250 limit) and 3 (combined coercion) do not collide, and deleting the case drops them too.
- Brewer v. West Irondequoit Central School District (No. 17, chunk 2). Collides with Parents Involved (OT2006). Decision: delete. Eisenberg Pt II already answered Parents Involved's objective question; deleting Brewer protects only the method question (whether individual students may be assigned by race), which is the part still open.
- Sterling v. Borough of Minersville (No. 59, chunk 5). Collides with the Lawrence, Windsor and Obergefell line. Decision: delete.
- Chen v. City of Houston (No. 72). Collides with Evenwel (OT2015). Decision: keep and decide; Evenwel becomes pre-decided.
- Tuan Anh Nguyen v. INS (No. 93). Collides with Morales-Santana (OT2016). Decision: keep and decide, including the remedy; Morales-Santana becomes an application if this Court reaches it.

Status: the three deletions (Daggett, Brewer and Sterling) are decided but not yet applied. They await confirmation after the Daggett note above, and until then all three remain on the OT2000 list. When applied, they remove the three from the OT2000 list (114 matters become 111, renumbered; chunk sizes become 11, 14, 14, 15, 13, 15, 14 and 15) and from the docket of granted lower-court cases (27 become 24). Chen and Nguyen are kept.

No other OT2000 entry touches Parents Involved or the orientation line. For later terms, avoid adding Parents Involved's own lower-court cases (Seattle, Louisville, and Comfort v. Lynn School Committee) or orientation vehicles such as Lofton v. Secretary of the Florida Department of Children and Families (Eleventh Circuit, 2004).

The consolidated proposed placement of the voting vehicles now appears in [section 6.2](#complete-docket). The questions in 3.9.8 remain matters for actual opinions; a planning recommendation does not record their adoption or add a historical Supreme Court grant.

### (c) Corrections that still apply

Corrections to carry forward, as far as they concern this area, with present status:

1. Shaw is fixed. The June 28, 1993 judgment rejects the territorial racial-design-only equal-protection theory. Do not later treat historical Shaw as controlling law.
2. Deleted progeny. Hays, Shaw II / Pope and Abrams stay off future case lists. The Hunt and Easley deletions are superseded (Hunt was decided in OT1998; Easley is on the OT2000 list). A genuinely independent later dispute gets its own lineage rather than inheriting a deleted caption automatically.
3. Reconstructed cases. Miller (OT1994) and Vera (OT1995) were run only as expressly counterfactual grants with revised questions and new lineage; done.
4. Predominance is evidence, not a trigger. It does not by itself create constitutional injury, a scrutiny tier or liability; that is now law (Shaw, Miller, Vera, Hunt). The earlier plan also said it does not by itself create standing; Miller Pt I-A and Vera recognize standing for voters personally assigned, and whether Stone narrows that is question D1.
5. Remove the abandoned racial framework. Do not use the old numbered lanes, the nonexclusive/discrete-benefit split, or any parallel standards section that would recreate them. Actual racial treatment is evaluated through lawful authority, objective support, operation, burdens, alternatives and continued justification.
6. Adarand. Superseded: the common standard is law (Stone for five), and the Stevens fallback never operated. Any statement that the fallback prevailed in OT1994 is wrong.
7. No branded replacement test. Do not give the Adarand reasoning a special doctrinal title; state the legal questions in ordinary terms and tie each to the source and problem it addresses.
8. VRA separation. Section 2 remains a statutory results inquiry where its enacted elements control. Race-conscious statutory implementation is not itself a constitutional injury. A constitutional challenge, statutory coverage, statutory validity, implementation, cause of action and remedy remain distinct.
9. School integration. Do not relabel voluntary integration as remediation when no remedial predicate exists; identify the prospective educational objective and test the actual racial means and burdens (now Eisenberg).
10. Chen. Retain the total-population question. The separate design-only Shaw theory is foreclosed unless the pleadings preserve an independently cognizable voting or equality injury.
11. Actual records. Do not copy favorable findings created by this Court's own rulings into historical cases, do not treat a proposed crossover district as already proven effective, and do not turn preliminary-injunction records into final merits records.
12. Procedural dependencies. Recheck the reconstructed Miller and Vera line, the Bossier sequence and the Adarand sequence after each of this Court's judgments. New reasoning does not authorize invention of the next record.

### (d) Location of case recommendations

The older OT2000 plans and the additional OT2001 equality and enforcement plans are preserved in [section 6.3](#carried-forward-plans). That location separates proposed answers from the question register and completed law. The full voting docket, including its historical and hypothetical vehicles, is in [section 6.2](#complete-docket).

## 6. Complete voting test and October Term docket plan

<a id="voting-plan"></a>

This is the planning section for proposed voting and districting decisions. It contains the **complete test**, its definitions and illustrative forms, and the **complete OT2001–OT2026 docket**, followed by older plans carried forward from this tracker. Case-specific recommendations belong here. The landmark entries and question lists above remain a record of questions, historical posture, and decisions already made.

**Read the section in this order:** identify the protected interest and the operation alleged; apply the relevant elements, proof, defense, and remedy; then use the dated case entry to determine what Stone could properly decide on that record. The docket follows a shared sequence: **history → questions → recommended disposition → reasoning → what the opinion must articulate**. The questions in the plan are drafting questions; unless expressly quoted, they are not transcriptions of a filed Question Presented. Stone is the only character assigned a proposed position.

**Authority and time.** A recommendation is not a recorded vote or a holding. Existing simulated decisions control their actual issues; a new formulation must be announced and defended in an appropriate case before a later opinion can treat it as law. Historical decisions supply real facts, procedures, arguments, and comparisons. They do not supply fictional votes. Later authorities may inform this planning document but may not be cited as already decided in an earlier simulated opinion. The completed-case ledger above retains its stated June 2000 cutoff; the supplied holdings used for the test include later simulation decisions. Before running an older entry, check the dated adjudication rather than rerunning a case already decided. Importing this plan does not amend a completed judgment.

**Selected approach.** The representation branch uses the recorded range under the prior lawful map, with proved continuity; reaggregation is ordinary proof, not a required method. There is no fixed election count or mandatory defeat under the new map. A lawful comparison may have an independent legal, institutional, or practical basis beyond the State's own criteria. The separate association branch protects actual association and concrete efforts to organize or participate, without a freestanding duration requirement. These choices protect voting and organizing access while requiring a specific injury, the appropriate purpose and causal proof, and a lawful remedy. They do not convert every adverse partisan outcome into liability. Segregate remains reserved; statutory claims keep their own elements.

**Navigation:** [Complete test](#complete-test) · [Operations and forms](#test-forms) · [Complete docket and term index](#complete-docket) · [Older carried-forward plans](#carried-forward-plans).

<a id="complete-test"></a>

### 6.1 The complete Independent Civic Deprivation Test

**Status of the formulation.** The rules below are a proposed doctrine, not adopted simulated holdings. Independently grounded lawful comparisons, the protective association rule, the emerging-electorate route, and present-membership standing form part of this formulation. Their inclusion does not make them existing precedent or an exact restatement of Foundation 15. The continuity rules and other expressly identified extensions retain their proposed status and require adoption in an appropriate simulated decision.

*A comprehensive proposed doctrine for racial and partisan injuries in elections and districting*

**Status and authority.** This is a proposed synthesis for the Stone-Zsela Supreme Court simulation. It is not, by its own force, an existing simulated holding or a statement of current real-world constitutional law. Its racial foundation comes from the supplied simulated decisions and standards register, especially *Shaw*, *Miller*, *Vera*, *Sinkfield*, *Rice*, *Adarand*, *Wilson*, *Brooks*, *Bossier*, *Voinovich*, *Easley*, *Growe*, *Holder*, *Reform Party*, and *Lopez*. The four established racial operations synthesize that record, although their complete unified formulation includes proposed choices about justification and pleading. **Segregate is fully defined but remains proposed. Foreclose and Entrench are likewise proposed partisan operations.** The historical cases named below provide support or comparison, not fictional simulated votes or holdings. No case is converted into simulated precedent merely by appearing here.

**Principal features.** The formulation includes seven central proposals; none becomes simulated precedent merely by appearing here. (1) Neither partisan operation requires a fixed number of elections. (2) Foreclose's representation branch is measured by loss of the capacity to elect across reasonably foreseeable electoral conditions, set by recorded swings. (3) Foreclose has a second, association branch. (4) Election history carries across a lawful predecessor map and a substantively equivalent replacement. (5) Segregate stays drafted and is reserved; no case on the current docket adopts it. (6) Racial Burden expressly includes deliberate fracture of a functioning coalition. (7) Exclude reaches racial bars to candidacy, and the racial operations protect voters of every race.

**How authorities are marked.** In the support paragraphs and the Appendix, a dagger (†) marks a decision that has its own entry in the simulated register; the simulated holding controls, not the real one. An unmarked case is a real decision from before July 2001 that the register already cites. An asterisk (\*) marks a real decision from before July 2001 that is carried over as supporting law but that the register does not yet cite; it should be confirmed against the simulation before it is relied on as authority. Decisions and scholarship after June 2001 are theory only and are listed separately in the Appendix.

The test has **one governing principle, one judicial procedure, two distinct substantive branches, and explicit overlap rules.** There is no single seven-operation list with a uniform justification rule.

**Navigation.** Part I supplies standing, proof, comparison, remedy, and definitions. Part II addresses racial Exclude, Mark, Burden, Dilute, and reserved Segregate. Part III distinguishes Foreclose's representation and association branches from Entrench's body-wide control inquiry. Part IV addresses direct partisan burdens, overlapping claims, illustrative pleading forms, and trial administration. The Appendix distinguishes simulated holdings, historical support, later comparison material, and proposed extensions.

---

#### Part I. Common Governing Rules

##### I. The controlling principle

Government may know about race and party, study them, predict political behavior, and take lawful account of the conditions they reveal. A legislature cannot draw election districts without considering political geography. Racial information may also help it understand exclusion, voting opportunities, communities, and statutory obligations. The mere weight given to race or party in a mapmaker's deliberations is not the constitutional injury.

**The injury is what the State made the characteristic do to a person's place in the political community.** A plaintiff must identify an *operation* of government that changes, officially marks, obstructs, or destroys a protected civic interest in a way the applicable constitutional rule forbids.

The distinction has two consequences.

First, **predominance is evidence, not a completed violation**. Proof that race was the most important districting consideration does not, without more, establish the racial claim recognized by the simulated *Shaw/Miller* line. Proof that partisanship dominated map design likewise does not alone establish partisan Foreclose or Entrench. A district may look bizarre or have a markedly racial or partisan population without operating on the plaintiff in a forbidden way.

Second, the characteristic matters. The Constitution treats racial restrictions on political membership differently from ordinary partisan competition. The racial and partisan branches therefore share the inquiry into *operation*, but not the same prohibited acts, necessary election history, constitutional sources, defenses, or standards of justification.

##### II. Protected civic interests

A plaintiff must name an interest the law protects, not merely dislike the map. The relevant interests may include:

1. **Eligibility:** the ability to register, vote, or participate in a public election or an election stage effectively controlled by the State.
2. **The official ballot:** participation without prohibited governmental labels, penalties, or official voting cues.
3. **Political access:** materially equal ability to register, nominate, petition, qualify candidates, seek lawful political remedies, or use government procedures.
4. **Effective electoral opportunity:** a group's real, legally cognizable chance to elect its preferred candidates, assessed under the specific racial or partisan theory asserted.
5. **Political association:** the ability to organize, nominate, and participate through an actual political association or concrete ongoing or ready-to-undertake efforts to form or participate in one, free of deliberate governmental obstruction. Racial Burden also protects the realistic coalition-building and participation opportunities specified in Section IX.
6. **Equal civic status:** freedom from certain state-imposed racial distinctions in political membership, including the proposed, narrowly defined injury of deliberate racial partition.
7. **Electoral reversibility:** under proposed partisan Entrench, voters' capacity to replace a governing majority through elections when support for that majority is withdrawn.

These are **not** rights to a particular district shape, racial composition, party composition, incumbent, historical boundary, election winner, proportional allocation of seats, or desirable attention from an elected representative. A group cannot plead a violation by saying only that its candidate lost or that the legislature refused to create a new favorable district.

##### III. The judicial sequence

The court proceeds in a fixed order. It does not decide the merits inside the standing inquiry or infer the applicable constitutional standard from the map's appearance.

**Stage 1, standing:** Is the plaintiff personally exposed to the challenged law, practice, assignment, or electoral structure? Is the asserted injury traceable to government action and likely to be redressed by the requested relief? Standing is a jurisdictional threshold. It is resolved before any ruling on the merits and is **not** an element of liability; no operation below lists personal exposure among its elements. Being a voter somewhere in the State, or living near an oddly shaped district, is not invariably enough. The simulated *Miller* and *Sinkfield* require a real personal connection.

**Stage 2, complaint:** Does the pleading allege facts identifying the plaintiff, a protected civic interest, and at least one specific prohibited operation? A complaint alleging only motive, predominance, unusual design, demographic concentration, partisan advantage, or an unfavorable seat tally fails. The needed facts vary by operation. Ordinary pleading standards govern; this is a substantive rule about what constitutes a claim, not a special heightened pleading code.

**Stage 3, proof of an operation:** Which operation does the State's rule, administrative practice, or districting arrangement perform, and has the plaintiff proved each of that operation's elements? An operation may be proved by direct records or circumstantial evidence. Where the operation has a purpose element, the plaintiff must prove it. Where it has an electoral-effect element, rhetoric and projections cannot replace the required effect.

**Stage 4, causation and lawful State answer:** Has the State rebutted a required element, established the claim-specific same-decision defense, or proved the justification permitted by that operation's particular rule? A categorical racial prohibition, a purpose-based wrong, the proposed partisan Foreclose defense, and Entrench's independent-lawful-constraints inquiry **do not share a single standard**.

**Stage 5, remedy:** What must be removed to cure the injury suffered by the proper plaintiffs? The court starts from the violation proved. It does not seize control of the entire map merely because some districts offend the Constitution.

###### What pleading means and what trial proof means

At the complaint stage, the plaintiff alleges concrete facts that, if proved, satisfy the claim. The plaintiff does not have to attach expert reports, prove every inference, produce a perfect alternative map, or establish a trial record before discovery. At summary judgment or trial, however, allegations cease to be enough. The plaintiff must establish purpose, actual operation, electoral history, causal inference, or material differential access **where the chosen operation requires them**. The State has the opportunity to contest those facts.

There is **no universal requirement** that an election be lost, that money be lost, that a second government act occur, or that an alternative map be drawn. But an operation-specific requirement still controls: racial Exclude and Mark can be complete upon imposition; Dilute requires actual impairment of opportunity; Foreclose's representation branch requires loss of capacity across the recorded range; its association branch requires its own concrete material impairment, without a no-win or recorded-range prerequisite; and Entrench requires retention of governing control across the recorded opposition-majority conditions. No operation requires a fixed number of elections. The general principle cannot erase these express elements.

###### Burden and standard of proof

The plaintiff bears the burden on every element of the operation pleaded and must prove each element by a preponderance of the evidence. Where this test assigns a showing to the State, the State bears that burden by the same standard. Those showings are the same-decision defense to a purpose-based claim, the simulated *Adarand* justification for an open racial classification, and the lawful-constraints answer to Entrench. No operation applies a heightened standard of proof, and none requires the State to disprove an element.

###### When a claim arises and when relief takes effect

A claim arises when its operation is complete. A concretely threatened material association impairment may also support prospective relief under ordinary standing, proof, and interim-relief rules; an abstract fear does not establish the threatened operation.

* **On imposition:** racial Exclude and Mark, and the direct partisan operations described in Part IV.
* **On application of the unequal condition:** racial Burden.
* **When the arrangement takes effect and its elements can be shown:** racial Dilute, the proposed Segregate, Foreclose, and Entrench. Completed elections under the challenged map are evidence, not a precondition.

A court asked for relief shortly before an election may allow that election to proceed under the challenged arrangement and order relief for the next one, where an immediate change would disrupt the conduct of the election. Real *Reynolds v. Sims* (1964) recognizes that equitable discretion. Deferral postpones the remedy. It does not excuse the violation.

Recognizing a claim before an election does not by itself entitle the plaintiff to an injunction before that election; the ordinary requirements for interim relief apply. After an election, the court reassesses timely, material evidence bearing on the deprivation and the continuing need for relief, and it modifies or dissolves relief when the applicable standard is no longer satisfied. If the affected electorate elects its preferred candidate under the challenged map in a condition within the defined recorded range, Foreclose's representation inability is disproved; calling that victory exceptional cannot preserve that claim. A victory does not by itself resolve a distinct association injury or Entrench's body-wide control question. Success under a remedial map neither automatically disproves the original injury nor automatically resolves the continuing need for relief.

##### IV. The common evidence rule

Courts may consider map shape, departures from county and municipal lines, census data, racial and party demographics, election returns, stated objectives, private drafting correspondence, the sequence of enactment, treatment of comparable communities, rejected alternatives, and the identity and actions of the mapmaker. A racial or partisan explanation can be both truthful and legally insufficient. An ostensibly neutral explanation may be pretextual.

**None of those facts is itself the prohibited operation.** Shape does not substitute for excluding a voter. Racial predominance does not substitute for proving intentional dilution. A sponsor's boast about obtaining five more seats does not substitute for Foreclose's lost capacity to elect, its distinct material association impairment, or Entrench's control retained against a voting majority. Conversely, once a prohibited operation is proved, an ordinary-looking map, a regular committee process, or the label *partisan* does not make it lawful.

For a claim that depends on a different map as a causal comparison, that map must have independent legal and practical roots. A plaintiff may not assume an ideal racial ratio or desired partisan majority and then call every map failing to produce it unlawful. The simulated *Holder* supplies this discipline. Counterfactual maps are evidence of causation, not new constitutional entitlements.

**Independently grounded lawful comparisons.** A comparison map must be lawful, workable, and grounded in legal, institutional, or practical considerations independent of the desired racial or partisan result. It must comply with every applicable legal requirement. Genuine, lawful, consistently applied State criteria receive appropriate weight but are not exclusive: a plaintiff may use other independently grounded lawful districting practices. Population equality and applicable federal statutes remain binding; contiguity, political subdivisions, and other practices receive the force their legal or practical basis supports. The court tests the genuineness, consistency, feasibility, and relevance of asserted criteria, including criteria advanced after litigation or applied selectively. An ideal party outcome, desired racial ratio, or more favorable seat total cannot itself supply the baseline. This rule governs causal comparisons in racial Dilute, Foreclose, and Entrench alike; it preserves legitimate State causation and same-decision defenses.

**Basis and status of the comparison rule.** Independently grounded lawful comparisons accord with Foundation 15's permission to consider several kinds of lawful comparison. Simulated *Holder*† supports a legally grounded, workable benchmark; real *Karcher v. Daggett* (1983) supports testing the genuineness and consistency of asserted policies. Neither is treated as having adopted every application of this proposed rule.

##### V. Common remedy and statutory boundary

Relief follows the **proved deprivation**, not the plaintiff's preferred political result. The State's lawful mapmaking authority has the first real opportunity to correct district lines, consistent with the simulated *Growe*. Relief generally reaches only the challenged practice, districts, and necessary neighboring adjustments, consistent with *Vera*, *Reform Party*, and *Holder*.

There are three different remedial acts:

* **Stop a practice:** remove an official racial or political label, end unequal registration requirements, or restore ordinary access to a political process.
* **Invalidate a boundary:** strike the unlawful line or enactment. Whether an earlier lawful boundary comes back depends on the governing law and the judgment; invalidation is not itself judicial map drawing.
* **Substitute new lines:** only when the lines are the means of the constitutional injury, the State has failed to supply a timely lawful cure, and the court's intervention is independently authorized and no broader than necessary. The simulated *Lopez* controls that last step.

Where racial and partisan wrongs overlap, a court crafts **one effective cure**, not two redundant maps or inconsistent mandates. No remedy assures a party control, awards a candidate a seat, guarantees racial balance, restores a vanished district as property, or requires a court-appointed commission as an automatic matter.

**Voting Rights Act independence.** Section 2 and any applicable Section 5 claim proceeds under its own statute, proof, and remedies. The Independent Civic Deprivation Test is **not a threshold gate** for the Voting Rights Act, and a failure under one constitutional operation does not defeat an independently valid statutory action. Conversely, possible statutory duties do not by themselves prove a constitutional deprivation under this test. The simulated *Shaw*, *Miller*, *Vera*, and *SCLC* preserve the distinction.

##### V-A. Defined terms

These definitions apply throughout the test.

**Operation.** A rule, official classification, administrative practice, or districting arrangement by which government makes race or party act on a protected civic interest. An operation is identified by what the State did to the plaintiff, never by the State's reasons alone.

**Design-only complaint.** A complaint that alleges motive, predominance, shape, demographic composition, partisan advantage, or a seat tally, and no operation.

**Substantial or motivating factor.** A purpose that actually contributed to the decision. It need not be the sole or primary purpose. Acting with knowledge of an effect is not acting because of it.

**Material.** Large enough to change a person's or a group's real ability to participate or to elect. A technical difference with no practical consequence is not material.

**Completed election.** A general election whose results are final, held under the map relevant to the proposition being proved: the prior lawful map, a qualifying predecessor under the proposed continuity rules, or the challenged map. A primary or special election counts only if it is the election that fills the seat. The court identifies which map governed each election and why its results are relevant.

**Complete election cycle.** For Entrench, the period in which every seat of the governing body has been filled once at a regular election. A body elected all at once completes a cycle at each general election. A staggered body completes a cycle only when all of its seats have been contested.

**Prior lawful map.** The last districting plan that actually governed a general election and was not adjudged unlawful. A plan that was enacted but never used, or that a court set aside, is not a prior lawful map. Two continuity rules apply. First, election history under the immediate lawful predecessor counts where the same precinct core carried over. Second, a map enacted to replace a challenged map is treated as the same map where it preserves the challenged assignments in substance, and the baseline remains the last lawful map before the alleged injury. Continuity must be proved, and an unlawful arrangement is never the baseline.

**Demonstrated opportunity.** A capacity to elect shown by completed elections under the prior lawful map, counted with the continuity rules, sufficient to establish that the electorate's success was real and not the product of one unusual year or one candidate's personal appeal. No fixed number of elections is required.

**Identifiable, cohesive electorate.** Ordinarily, the voters of the precincts that formed the core of a district under the prior lawful map and that, on recorded returns, supported the same candidates by consistent majorities. Under the express emerging-electorate alternative in Section XIV-E, the plaintiff instead identifies the relevant geographic electorate, proves its cohesion from reliable evidence, and proves the opportunity required by that exception; a previously exercised district opportunity is not assumed. The electorate is identified by geography and voting evidence. A party label alone does not define it, and neither does a city or county line.

**Member of an electorate.** A person with present membership in the identified affected electorate, personal exposure to the challenged assignment, and participation or a concrete intention to participate in the political activity at issue. Registration records, residence, primary participation, a sworn statement, or comparable evidence may establish the relevant connection. The group's historical electoral opportunity is distinct from the individual plaintiff's standing: she need not have resided, been registered, or voted there during the historical elections relied on. The same rule applies to the emerging-electorate route. Traceability and redressability remain necessary. The secret ballot is no obstacle, and no plaintiff must disclose a particular vote. This present-membership rule is a protective proposed rule, not an existing simulated holding.

**Reasonably foreseeable electoral conditions.** The range of electoral conditions actually recorded in the area during the life of the prior lawful map, counted with the continuity rules. The range is set by recorded swings in completed elections. It is not a forecast, and no fixed band of percentage points defines it.

**Loss of capacity.** After reassignment, the electorate cannot elect its preferred candidate in any district to which its precincts were assigned under any condition within the reasonably foreseeable range. A reduced margin, a harder race, or a district the electorate would win under some condition within the range is not a loss of capacity.

**Protected political association or activity.** An actual political association, including an informal coalition or organized body of voters, or concrete ongoing or ready-to-undertake efforts to form or participate in one. Relevant activity includes recruiting candidates, seeking nominations, coordinating organizing, and participating together in the political process. A formal organization is unnecessary. The activity or concrete plan must exist independently of the lawsuit; shared party preference, shared racial identity, an abstract wish to associate, or dissatisfaction with electoral leverage alone is insufficient.

**One-sided purpose.** For Foreclose, the aim of taking an electorate's capacity to elect or materially impairing protected political association or activity, in order to penalize the affected voters for political participation or benefit their opponents. Political awareness or a desire for ordinary electoral advantage alone does not establish the purpose to cause the specified injury.

**Even-handed policy.** A districting rule established before the challenged map and applied alike to every party's electorates, including those of the mapmaker's own party. An even-handed policy is not a one-sided purpose even when it deliberately changes existing opportunities.

**Governing party or coalition.** A party or alliance whose seats determine control of an elected body and that exists independently of the lawsuit.

**Reliably measured vote.** For Entrench, a vote total that rests on votes actually cast in contested seats and that accounts for uncontested seats through evidence disclosed and tested in the litigation. If the claimed majority exists only on untested assumptions about how voters in uncontested seats would have voted, it is not reliably measured.

##### V-B. Pre-2001 support for the common rules

* **Standing as a separate threshold:** the simulated *Miller*† and *Sinkfield*†.
* **Dismissal of design-only complaints:** the simulated *Shaw*†, *Miller*†, *Vera*†, *Hunt v. Cromartie*†, and *Easley*†.
* **Purpose proved by direct or circumstantial evidence:** *Village of Arlington Heights v. Metropolitan Housing Development Corp.* (1977) and *Personnel Administrator v. Feeney* (1979); the simulated *Miller*† and *Easley*†.
* **Comparison maps with independent roots:** the simulated *Holder*†; *Karcher v. Daggett* (1983).
* **Remedy limited to the violation, with the State acting first:** *Reynolds v. Sims* (1964); *White v. Weiser*\* (1973); *Upham v. Seamon*\* (1982); the simulated *Growe*†, *Vera*†, *Holder*†, *Reform Party*†, and *Lopez*†.
* **Independence of the Voting Rights Act:** the simulated *Shaw*†, *Miller*†, *Vera*†, and *SCLC*†.

The standard of proof and the accrual rules stated in Section III are proposed. No decision in either list states them in these terms.

---

#### Part II. Racial Operations

##### VI. The racial branch's premise

The racial branch addresses what the State made **race** do to a protected incident of political membership. It is grounded principally in the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment, with the Fifteenth Amendment and other independent election rules supplying additional or overlapping prohibitions when applicable. The doctrine does not displace the simulation's separate, common **substantial-justification** standard for government racial classifications established in simulated *Adarand*.

The five operations are **Exclude, Mark, Burden, Dilute, and Segregate**. The first four consolidate recognized constitutional injuries and preserved cases; their precise, unified proof and justification instructions include proposed refinements. Segregate is a specified new category whose authority the simulated Court has yet to adopt.

**Every race.** The racial operations protect voters of every race. A white voter and a Black voter have the same protection against an actual racial deprivation, and neither has a right to a district of a preferred racial composition. Real *United States v. Brown* (5th Cir. 2009), theory only, illustrates the point under the statute.

| Operation | What race does | When the racial wrong is complete | Proposed rule for the State |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Exclude** | Determines who belongs to the public electorate | When exclusion is imposed | No justification once the prohibited racial franchise condition is proved |
| **Mark** | Officially designates voters or candidates inside political participation | When the designation is imposed | No defense of symmetry or voter information; categorical rule proposed |
| **Burden** | Makes the terms of access materially unequal | When material unequal treatment is established | Open classification: simulated *Adarand*; intentional denial: no separate justification after causation |
| **Dilute** | Purposefully impairs a group's effective electoral opportunity | When discriminatory purpose and material effect are proved | Same-decision causation defense where applicable; no separate justification for established intentional dilution |
| **Segregate** | Uses coordinated boundaries to partition racial communities for separation's own sake | When deliberate partition is actually implemented and personally reaches the claimant | Proposed categorical prohibition, with a same-decision causation defense |

The operations may **overlap**. A plan could both dilute a community's effective voting strength and deliberately separate communities. The plaintiff must prove the elements of each claim, but overlapping claims never produce double relief.

##### VII. Exclude: racial control of the electorate

**Protected interest:** eligibility to vote and participate in public electoral decision-making.

**Plaintiff must prove:**

1. A public election or state-controlled stage of the election, including an officially controlled primary.
2. A rule, qualification, boundary, or practice that leaves particular persons outside that electorate or prevents their lawful participation.
3. A racial criterion operative in the rule or established through its actual purpose and administration.

**Threshold, not an element:** the plaintiff must be a person the exclusion actually reaches. That is decided as standing under Section III.

**Candidacy.** Exclude reaches a racial criterion for who may be voted for as well as for who may vote. A racial bar to candidacy limits every voter's choice by race, and no proof that the excluded candidate would have won is needed. This extension is proposed. The simulated *Rice*† reserved the question.

The State may contest those predicates: perhaps the person remains eligible, the boundary serves a genuinely nonracial criterion, or the alleged primary is not within the claimed state-action doctrine. Once the actual racial exclusion is established, it cannot be excused because the affected public office supposedly serves a particular racial community, because the remaining voters possess equal ballots, or because the election's result might have been unchanged.

**Completion:** upon imposition of the operative exclusion. The person need not first lose an election, try unsuccessfully to vote, or lose money.

**Complaintant's pleading:** identify the election, original eligibility or participation route, challenged rule or boundary, the plaintiff's removal, and concrete facts making racial operation plausible. A racial disparity alone is evidence; the operative racial boundary must be alleged.

**Example:** the city redraws its limits to place almost every Black neighborhood outside the electorate while retaining comparable white neighborhoods. A former eligible municipal voter shows the boundary, the changed electorate, legislative records, and an operative racial criterion. The municipality may prove that race did not cause the exclusion; it may not justify racial disenfranchisement after the fact.

**Remedy:** invalidate or enjoin the exclusion. Where a boundary statute is invalidated, the judgment restores lawful eligibility under the applicable boundary law. A judge does not draw a fresh map merely by setting aside an unlawful enactment.

**Pre-2001 support:** *Guinn v. United States*\* (1915), which struck down a grandfather clause; *Nixon v. Herndon*\* (1927), which struck down a racial exclusion from a primary under the Fourteenth Amendment; *Smith v. Allwright* (1944) and *Terry v. Adams* (1953), which reached primaries run by parties; *Gomillion v. Lightfoot* (1960), in which a boundary accomplished the exclusion; and the simulated *Rice v. Cayetano*† (2000). *Gomillion* and *Rice* rest on the Fifteenth Amendment. *Nixon* supplies the Fourteenth Amendment anchor, so the equal-protection basis is not assumed from the other two.

##### VIII. Mark: official racial designation in participation
