**Protected interest:** a ballot and state-administered electoral process free of governmental racial participation cues and separated racial participation records.

**Plaintiff must prove:**

1. An **official governmental act**.
2. A racial designation attached to an identifiable voter or candidate.
3. The designation's actual placement **inside the State's machinery of political participation**, whether as a cue printed for voters or as racially separate registration or participation lists.

**Threshold, not an element:** the plaintiff must be a candidate, voter, or participant the designation actually reaches. That is decided as standing under Section III.

**Completion:** upon imposition. Every candidate may be marked and still each is subjected to the governmental racial cue. Mark does **not** require proof that a candidate was given an inferior office, voters made a different choice, one race received a more favorable label, or an actual election winner changed.

This is the genuinely distinctive *status* claim. Under preserved real *Anderson v. Martin*, the State cannot write a candidate's race into the official ballot as a prompt for voting. The real *Tancil v. Woolls* summary affirmance of *Hamm v. Virginia State Board of Elections* provides support for differentiating separate racial voter-participation lists from permissible race notation for unrelated statistical recordkeeping.

**What is not a Mark:** census analysis, aggregate racial statistics used to check wait times or legal compliance, records maintained for nondiscriminatory administration without segregated participation rights, and a candidate's voluntary statement about identity. Knowing the voters' race is not the same as officially telling them to participate as racial categories.

**State's answer:** contest whether the alleged mark is official or functionally inside the electoral process. Once the tightly defined Mark exists, the proposed rule admits **no separate voter-information or equal-application justification**. That categorical treatment is an express proposed extension, not a justification standard already announced in the simulation register.

**Example:** official ballot papers identify every candidate by race. The State claims all races are treated identically and voters could ignore the labels. Those answers fail under the proposed Mark rule because the injury is the government's insertion of race as a cue into the official choice.

**Remedy:** remove the label, integrate separated participation records, and preserve lawful nondiscriminatory administrative records. No map need be changed.

**Pre-2001 support:** *Anderson v. Martin* (1964), which struck down racial labels on ballots although they applied to every candidate; and *Tancil v. Woolls*\* (1964), a summary affirmance of *Hamm v. Virginia State Board of Elections*, which set aside racially separated voting records while leaving statistical notation in place. A summary affirmance adopts the judgment and not every statement of the court below. Neither case states the categorical no-justification rule, which is proposed.

##### IX. Burden: unequal access to political procedures

**Protected interest:** materially equal terms for entering and using the electoral and political process.

**Plaintiff must prove:**

1. A real political process the plaintiff or affected electorate seeks to use: registration, nomination, candidacy, petition, access to local decision-making, or another protected procedure.
2. Terms of access materially worse for the claimant's race, or for access to policies and remedies concerning racial inequality, as measured against the **actual ordinary process**.
3. Racial operation by an express classification, proved discriminatory purpose in application, or selective structural relocation of the avenue available for addressing racial discrimination.
4. A material restriction on the opportunity to participate, not just a technical distinction with no meaningful burden.

The comparator is functional. For a registrar, compare actual document demands and processing opportunities at similarly situated offices. For a political-process restructuring under simulated *Wilson*, compare the procedure used for racial-remedial policies with the procedure used for other lawful policies. A legislature may write a facially neutral rule that moves decisions about racial remedies from city councils to a remote constitutional-amendment process. **The discriminatory structure need not name an individual voter on its face.**

**Two different justifications must remain separate.** An explicit governmental racial classification that changes competitive access receives the simulated *Adarand* standard: the State must show an important lawful objective and a substantial, supported fit including relevant burdens and reasonable alternatives. A deliberately discriminatory denial of materially equal access has no independent lawful justification once intent, materiality, and causation are proved. A State can still contest that it acted for the prohibited purpose or establish the applicable same-decision defense.

**Coalition and association form.** Burden also reaches government's deliberate use of racial identity to split an actual political association, or to impair materially a community's realistic coalition-building or participation. The claim does not depend on showing that some other map would let the community elect its first-choice candidate. The plaintiff must identify the affected voters, the governmental arrangement, the actual association or realistic coalition-building or participation affected, discriminatory purpose or unequal legal treatment, a concrete mechanism of material impairment, and causation. An already functioning organization is not a universal prerequisite to a claim of impaired realistic coalition-building or participation. Breaking an operating nomination coalition, dividing an organized community to obstruct its participation, and imposing discriminatory access to elected officials are examples of mechanisms; inability to nominate is one example of impairment and is not required. Shared racial identity alone does not show a coalition, improper intent alone does not show impairment, and dissatisfaction with electoral leverage is not an injury. This form creates no statutory influence claim under Section 2. It is proposed, follows Stone's preferred development in Foundation 15, and real *White v. Regester* (1973) supports only its attention to actual access to the political process.

**Example, administrative:** a registrar requires two additional documents from applicants in majority-Black precincts despite neutral state law. Applicants show the actual difference and its racial design or operation. The remedy orders ordinary treatment.

**Example, structural:** ordinary programs need a city-council vote, but only programs addressing racial discrimination must pass a statewide constitutional amendment. The claim concerns selective obstruction of the political avenue, not entitlement to enact any particular racial-remedial program.

**State's answer:** deny the difference, establish immateriality, contest racial purpose, or satisfy *Adarand* where an otherwise permissible open classification is alleged. The court must not assume that every hard legislative procedure is discriminatory.

**Remedy:** remove the unequal access condition, restore the ordinary procedure, or correct the causal district lines when those lines materially impair protected association, realistic coalition-building, or participation. Any boundary remedy follows Section V, including the State's first opportunity to cure. Do not order voters, legislators, or city councils to approve the plaintiff's favored policy.

**Pre-2001 support:** *Yick Wo v. Hopkins* (1886), on discriminatory administration of a neutral law; *McLaurin v. Oklahoma State Regents* (1950), on formal access under unequal terms; *Louisiana v. United States*\* (1965), on discretionary registration tests; *Hunter v. Erickson* (1969) and *Washington v. Seattle School District No. 1* (1982), on restructured political processes; *White v. Regester* (1973), on unequal access to the political process; and the simulated *Wilson*† and *Adarand*†. Several arise in other institutional settings and are support by analogy, not districting holdings.

##### X. Dilute: intentional impairment of effective opportunity

**Protected interest:** an affected racial electorate's meaningful opportunity to participate in elections and elect its preferred candidates. This is opportunity, not a guaranteed victory or entitlement to proportional seats.

**Plaintiff must prove:**

1. An identifiable affected group and a meaningful electoral opportunity grounded in real voting conditions.
2. Racial discriminatory purpose that was a **substantial or motivating factor** in the State's adoption or operation of the challenged measure. The State must have acted at least partly **because of** the racial impact, not merely with knowledge of it.
3. **Actual material impairment** of the group's effective opportunity caused by the challenged boundary or practice. Historical election returns, cohesion, voting patterns, and reasonable comparisons may establish this; an individual candidate's loss by itself does not.
4. A sufficient connection between the discriminatory governmental act, its effect, and the plaintiffs whose effective opportunity is injured.

**State's answers:** establish the asserted racial purpose was not a motivating factor, show that the effective opportunity remains, offer legitimate political explanations as evidence on disputed motive, or prove under the purpose-based same-enactment framework that the materially same action would have occurred anyway. Under simulated *Easley*, *partisan* is **not a safe harbor** once racial purpose and material injury are proved. Neither is it automatic proof of a separate partisan Foreclose claim.

**Completion:** when racial purpose and actual adverse opportunity effect are established. No claimant must personally be a defeated candidate, identify a guaranteed winning map, or prove a separate racial ballot marking. A completed election may be important evidence, but neither this racial operation nor Foreclose requires a fixed number of elections. Their injury standards remain distinct: Dilute requires actual material impairment; Foreclose's representation branch requires loss of capacity across the defined recorded range.

**Proof before an election.** Dilute may be proved before an election is held under the challenged arrangement, from recorded voting patterns and lawful comparisons. Foreclose may likewise be proved without a completed election under the challenged map, subject to its distinct elements. Racial awareness or purpose alone is not forbidden: Dilute requires a discriminatory purpose to impair effective racial electoral opportunity, an actual material adverse effect, and causation. Foreclose requires its prohibited one-sided purpose and the injury of the particular branch. A pre-election claim must establish those elements and address the applicable State answer; motive alone never supplies the missing injury.

**Example:** legislative emails expressly describe splitting a cohesive Black community into three districts so it cannot elect its candidate. Returns and geographically realistic alternatives support a serious loss of that community's prior opportunity. The defendant calls it a partisan plan. The court tests that claim against the evidence; it does not let the partisan word erase a proved racial motive.

**Causation and justification:** the State bears the burden of a genuine same-decision showing when that defense applies under simulated *Brooks*, informed by *Mt. Healthy*, *Hunter v. Underwood*, and *Arlington Heights*. If that showing fails and the elements of intentional dilution are established, there is **no second freestanding justification** for purposefully taking away racial electoral opportunity. This no-separate-justification statement is part of the proposed unified test.

**Remedy:** let the State restore lawful opportunity in the affected districts, changing necessary neighbors but not the whole State without a demonstrated need. Do not require a racial quota, a particular candidate, or a guaranteed result.

**Pre-2001 support:** *White v. Regester* (1973); *Arlington Heights* (1977), on proving purpose from circumstantial evidence; *Mt. Healthy City School District v. Doyle* (1977), on the same-decision defense; *Personnel Administrator v. Feeney* (1979), distinguishing action because of an effect from action despite it; *City of Mobile v. Bolden* (1980), requiring purpose; *Rogers v. Lodge*\* (1982), sustaining a finding of purposeful dilution; *Hunter v. Underwood* (1985); and the simulated *Voinovich*†, *Bossier*†, *Brooks*†, and *Easley*†. The opinions differ in status and reach, and the list does not mean all said the same thing. The rule that no separate justification survives a proved claim is proposed.

##### XI. Segregate: purposeful partition of racial political communities

**Status:** a fully drafted **fifth racial operation, proposed but not yet an authoritative simulated holding**. The case that squarely presents it would allow the Court to adopt, narrow, or reject it. The simulation's existing *Shaw/Miller/Vera* judgments did not establish that particular claim on their records, and they cannot be rewritten as findings of benign motive. **The category is reserved.** No case adopts it, a record of racial percentages or targets does not by itself present it, and the elements below stay drafted for a properly presented case that requires them.

**Protected interest:** equal civic membership, including freedom from governmental organization of neighboring electorates into separate racial political communities **for the sake of racial separation itself**. This interest gives no person a right to live in a district of any chosen racial percentage.

**Plaintiff must prove three elements:**

1. **Separation objective.** A substantial or motivating purpose of the challenged governmental arrangement was to keep racial groups in distinct electoral constituencies *as an end in itself*. An opportunity district, a plan correcting proved dilution, or a plan informed by racial demographics does not satisfy the element without this separative objective.
2. **Coordinated regional partition.** The State intentionally used a connected set of district boundaries to sort racial communities across the relevant districting region. The region can be two adjacent districts, a municipality, a county, or a broader plan. The plaintiff need not prove a statewide conspiracy. One district's composition, considered alone, is never enough.
3. **Actual separative operation.** The coordinated boundaries substantially implement the objective in real political assignments. There is no magic numerical threshold, no required near-total percentage, and no inference of liability from unusually high racial concentration standing alone.

**Threshold, not an element:** the plaintiff must be assigned to and governed through the challenged partition, with injury traceable to the arrangement and redressable by a cure. That is decided as standing under Section III.

**Limiting rule:** the wrong alleged must be the State's completed organization of citizens into separate racial political constituencies. A complaint that race predominated, that a district is oddly shaped, or that its demographics are objectionable does not state this claim, however it is labeled.

**State's answers:** dispute the separative purpose, the coordinated regional operation, or actual implementation, or contest the plaintiff's standing. Demonstrate that districting responded instead to genuine electoral opportunity, lawful Voting Rights Act compliance, consistent political and geographic criteria, or residential concentrations that the State did not create. As a proposed extension of the simulated *Brooks* causation framework, it may prove it would have drawn materially the same lawful lines without the separative objective.

**Justification:** no free-standing justification survives a proved segregation objective and the rejection of the same-decision defense. The simulated *Adarand* rejects racial allocation **for its own sake** as a lawful governmental objective. That statement helps explain why the objective is impermissible; it does **not**, by itself, supply the independent injury that existing *Miller* requires. This proposed fifth operation supplies and limits that separate injury.

**Example, actionable if adopted:** a legislature deliberately draws a city's four adjacent districts so that two predominantly Black communities vote separately from two predominantly white communities. Drafting instructions direct staff to keep the communities from sharing constituencies, and the completed map does that. A personally assigned resident identifies the relevant regional partition and its effect. She need not prove that her single vote has less weight or that her candidate already lost.

**Counterexample:** a legislature creates one majority-Black district to address an established Section 2 opportunity problem. It considers race extensively. On the record presented, there is no proof of an objective of racial separation for its own sake or a coordinated partition of the neighboring region. Race may have predominated, yet the proposed Segregate claim still fails.

**Remedy:** the lawful mapmaker redraws the affected regional arrangement without the unlawful purpose. Courts may not demand racial mixing percentages or proportionality. Judicial drawing remains subject to the State-first rule and the simulated *Lopez* necessity limits.

**Pre-2001 support:** *Brown v. Board of Education* (1954); *Gomillion v. Lightfoot* (1960); *Wright v. Rockefeller* (1964); *Keyes v. School District No. 1*\* (1973); and the simulated *Adarand*† list of impermissible objectives. No decision holds that purposeful partition of electoral districts is unconstitutional. That would be the new holding.

**How those cases bear on the claim:** real *Brown v. Board of Education* and *Keyes v. School District No. 1* show why geographical administration cannot always immunize intentional separation, but they are school-assignment cases and cannot be mechanically treated as districting holdings. *Wright v. Rockefeller* did not establish this broad rule; Justice Douglas's dissent articulates the competing concern. Real *Gomillion* shows how territorial lines can themselves be the mechanism of an exclusionary constitutional injury.

##### XII. Racial pleading and justification summary

The racial plaintiff has no general duty to establish **more** than the particular operation requires. She must **identify which operation** she is alleging and plead its factual basis.

An Exclude complaint identifies a racial electorate rule or operative boundary. A Mark complaint identifies an official designation in election administration. A Burden complaint identifies a material access differential and the racial mechanism or purpose. A Dilute complaint identifies deliberate racial impairment of effective group opportunity. A Segregate complaint identifies the purpose of separation, the coordinated region, substantial implementation, and personal assignment.

A claim consisting of **only** an unusually shaped majority-minority district and a legislative admission that race was decisive remains dismissible. No numerical racial proportion automatically changes the result. This preserves simulated *Shaw*, *Miller*, and *Vera*.

---

#### Part III. Partisan Operations

##### XIII. The partisan branch's different starting point

Party preference is not race. Legislative districting inevitably has political effects. Real *Gaffney v. Cummings*\* permits a State to consider political fairness and the parties' electoral strengths. The doctrine leaves room for competitive districting, protection of incumbents, use of election returns, political negotiation, and lawful, even-handed fairness policies.

The partisan branch nonetheless protects the ability of voters to **choose** representatives and, for governing bodies, to **replace** the majority in power. The State crosses the proposed constitutional line when its district lines accomplish one of two operations:

* **Foreclose:** single out voters for their political participation and either take away an identifiable electorate's capacity to elect across reasonably foreseeable electoral conditions, or materially impair protected political association or activity as defined in Section V-A.
* **Entrench:** place control of an elected governing body beyond the reach of a voting majority across reasonably foreseeable electoral conditions.

Neither operation makes political motive unconstitutional in the abstract. A map is not invalid merely because one party hopes to gain seats, draws safe districts, protects incumbents, declines to create a new opposition district, adopts a mid-decade map, or wins more seats than its statewide vote percentage. **Foreclose and Entrench are prohibited results of particular operations, not a general fairness tribunal.**

The two operations have different constitutional bases. Foreclose rests on the First Amendment's protection of political participation and association, applied to the States through the Fourteenth Amendment; congressional applications additionally invoke Article I. Entrench is proposed as a Fourteenth Amendment interest in **electoral reversibility** in an elected governing body.

###### Distinct measures for representation, association, and governing control

The proposals protect different interests. Foreclose's representation branch asks whether the affected electorate can elect in any destination district under any condition in the defined recorded range. Its association branch asks whether the lines materially impair, or concretely threaten material impairment of, protected political association or activity; it does not require inability to elect or nominate, a freestanding showing of durability, or proof across that electoral range. Entrench asks whether a voting majority can replace the governing majority of the body across the relevant opposition-majority conditions.

* **The electoral range**, where applicable, is the range of electoral conditions actually recorded in the area during the life of the prior lawful map, counted with the proposed continuity rules in Section V-A.
* **No mechanical test decides the question.** No fixed number of elections, seat percentage, symmetry score, or simulation is required or sufficient.
* **A claim may be proved before an election** is held under the challenged map, through reliable evidence of every element of its branch. Completed elections under the challenged map are relevant evidence and are never a precondition. An association claim rests on proof of its own impairment, not a required electoral loss.
* **Unsupported forecasts are insufficient.** A sponsor's stated goal, a bare prediction of voting behavior, or an unsupported projected seat count does not establish an operation. Reliable inference from recorded votes and other tested evidence is permissible, including a supported assessment of how the challenged lines operate across the recorded range. The court must explain the inference and address material limitations; the distinction is between supported proof and speculation, not between observed new-map elections and all counterfactual evidence.

**What defeats each proposed injury.** An electorate that could still elect its preferred candidate in any destination district under some condition within the recorded range has no Foreclose representation claim. That fact does not defeat a separately proved association injury. Nor does an opposition candidate's victory in one district answer Entrench: that operation concerns governing control of the whole body. A reduced margin, a district moved from safe to competitive, or an unfavorable seat total, standing alone, establishes none of the three injuries.

These partisan rules do not govern immediate burdens from ballot labels, exclusions, or unequal access rules. Those use the direct Exclude, Mark, or Burden framework in Part IV.

##### XIV. Foreclose: one-sided destruction of capacity or association

###### A. Scope, right, and constitutional basis

Foreclose applies to **congressional and state legislative districting**, post-census and mid-decade, and to maps adopted by a legislature, commission, or referendum. The protected interest is freedom from being penalized for political participation through the loss of representation or association. No electorate owns a district, an incumbent, or a past result.

Keeping congressional claims in Foreclose is a substantive limit and not only a label. Entrench has no purpose element, and Foreclose does.

###### B. Two branches

**Representation branch.** The State took away an electorate's capacity to elect across reasonably foreseeable electoral conditions.

**Association branch.** Through a concrete mechanism in the districting arrangement, the State materially impairs, or concretely threatens material impairment of, an actual political association or concrete ongoing or ready-to-undertake efforts to form or participate in one. A formal organization, inability to elect or nominate, and a separate durability threshold are not required.

A plaintiff may plead either or both. Each has its own elements.

###### C. What the plaintiff must allege in a complaint

For the representation branch the complaint identifies the electorate and either the completed elections showing its demonstrated opportunity or the express emerging-electorate alternative in Section XIV-E, including facts showing cohesion and a real opportunity established through independently grounded lawful comparisons under Section IV. It identifies the reassignment that split or submerged the electorate, facts showing that it cannot elect in any of its new districts across the recorded range, facts suggesting a one-sided purpose, and facts connecting the loss to the lines. It need not allege a completed election under the challenged map. Invoking the emerging alternative does not dispense with the remaining elements.

For the association branch the complaint identifies the actual association or concrete ongoing or ready-to-undertake associational activity, what the affected voters do or are concretely prepared to do, the mechanism by which the lines obstruct it, the actual or concretely threatened material impairment, and facts suggesting prohibited one-sided purpose and causation. Formal incorporation, inability to win or nominate, and a fixed period of obstruction need not be alleged.

A complaint that alleges only a sponsor's goal of gaining seats, a vanished district, a defeated incumbent, a reduced margin, or a seat share out of line with votes does not state either branch.

###### D. Standing

The plaintiff must be personally connected to the electorate or protected association or activity and to the challenged assignment, and must show that an appropriate redraw would give meaningful relief. Membership in the electorate is shown as Section V-A provides. A statewide party supporter with no such connection has no standing merely because she prefers the earlier map. The court decides this before the merits.

###### E. Elements of the representation branch

**Element 1, a demonstrated opportunity.** Under the prior lawful map, counted with the continuity rules, an identifiable, cohesive electorate elected its preferred candidate in completed elections sufficient to show the capacity was real. No fixed number of elections is required. One unusual year or one candidate's personal appeal is not enough.

*Emerging electorates — protective route.* An electorate without a demonstrated opportunity may proceed by proving a real electoral opportunity through a lawful, workable comparison independently grounded as Section IV requires, in addition to cohesion and every other element. The comparison must connect to the actual geographically identified community and reliable voting or participation evidence, not an electorate invented to produce a desired outcome. A map that merely could have been drawn more favorably does not supply the baseline.

**Protective extension and its limits.** This formulation adopts the emerging-electorate route to protect voting access and prevent discriminatory obstruction. It remains proposed new law, not an adopted simulated holding or a result compelled by Foundation 15 or simulated *Holder*. It extends the ordinary previously exercised baseline without lowering the representation-injury threshold: inability to elect in every destination district under every condition in the already defined historical range remains necessary. The plaintiff must establish the counterfactual opportunity and a grounded connection between the actual community and that historical evidence; emerging status neither expands the range nor supplies missing proof.

**Element 2, loss of capacity.** After reassignment, the electorate cannot elect its preferred candidate in any district to which its precincts were assigned under any condition within the reasonably foreseeable range. The ordinary proof is to take each completed election in the range and re-add its recorded precinct votes under the new lines. That method is not mandatory. Completed elections under the challenged map and other reliable evidence may establish or rebut the loss.

**Element 3, prohibited one-sided purpose.** A substantial or motivating aim was to take the electorate's opportunity in order to penalize it or to benefit its opponents. The use of political data without that aim does not satisfy the element, and neither does an even-handed policy. For a referendum, purpose is taken from the measure's stated objective and its drafters.

**Element 4, causation.** The challenged lines materially caused the loss. The plaintiff must distinguish the effect of the lines from changes in voter preference, population, or candidates; the mere existence of those other influences does not require them to be nonexistent or make sole causation an element. Comparison maps satisfy the independently grounded lawful-comparison rule in Section IV. The State's same-decision defense remains separately available.

###### F. Elements of the association branch

1. **Protected political association or activity**, as defined in Section V-A: an actual association or concrete ongoing or ready-to-undertake efforts to form or participate in one. A formal organization is unnecessary; shared political identity or an abstract wish to associate is insufficient.
2. **A concrete mechanism** in the districting arrangement that obstructs that activity, such as dividing an organizing base in a way that materially disrupts candidate recruitment, nomination efforts, or coordinated participation. An ordinary district split is not itself proof of obstruction, and inability to elect or nominate is not required.
3. **Actual or concretely threatened material impairment**, established under ordinary proof rules. The court assesses seriousness, timing, persistence, and repetition as evidence of the actual burden. No freestanding durability element or fixed waiting period applies: temporary obstruction at a critical registration, nomination, organizing, or voting stage can materially impair participation. Reduced leverage, disappointment, lost morale, or inconvenience without a concrete material restriction is insufficient. A speculative future difficulty is not a concretely threatened impairment.
4. **Prohibited one-sided purpose.** A substantial or motivating aim was to impair the protected association or activity to penalize political participation or benefit the affected voters' opponents. The representation branch's requirement of an aim to take away electoral opportunity is not an additional association element.
5. **Causation.** The challenged lines materially cause the actual or concretely threatened impairment; the plaintiff must identify how they do so. Ordinary political disagreement or voluntary loss of interest does not establish that connection. The State retains the same-decision defense below.

**Protective refinement and its limits.** To protect voting access and prevent discriminatory obstruction, this formulation does not impose a separate duration threshold and protects concrete efforts to form or join an association as well as existing associations. This is an express refinement of Foundation 15's substantial-and-durable language, not an exact restatement or an already adopted simulated holding. The requirement of concrete material impairment remains. Electoral victory may be relevant evidence, but does not automatically defeat a distinct association injury.

###### G. The State's same-decision defense

The State defeats either branch by proving, **by a preponderance of the evidence**, that it would have adopted **materially the same reassignment without the one-sided purpose**. Reasons that can carry the defense include population equality, a seat lost in reapportionment, a real Voting Rights Act obligation, consistently applied geographic criteria, and an **established, even-handed statewide fairness policy**, including one that deliberately changes existing opportunities. A merely conceivable reason, a label adopted after suit, the fact of a census, voter approval, or another State's conduct does not carry it. The absence of a new census does not by itself defeat the defense, and its presence does not establish it. Once the defense fails, no further justification is available.

###### H. Relief and examples

The State redraws the affected districts and necessary neighbors first. The new lines need not restore the old district or guarantee a result. Relief before and after an election follows the rules in Section III.

**Plaintiff wins before an election, on the stated assumptions.** A district has elected the minority party's candidate through five elections under the prior map. A mid-decade plan disperses its precincts among three districts, and drafting records state the aim of ending that electorate's ability to elect. Re-adding the recorded votes from each of the five elections shows its candidate losing in all three districts every time. Assume that reliable evidence supports inability across the full defined recorded range, not merely at the five sampled outcomes; that the electorate, purpose, and map-causation elements are proved; and that the State fails its same-decision defense. Liability is then established; whether an injunction issues before the election depends on the ordinary requirements. Five is illustrative, not a required election count.

**No claim.** The same facts, but in one of the five recorded elections the electorate's candidate would have carried one of the new districts. Democratic correction remains available to it, and the representation branch fails. The association branch remains open if protected political association or activity was actually or concretely threatened with material impairment through the lines, with its other elements proved.

**State wins on the defense.** A commission applying a published rule that keeps counties whole produces the same loss, and proves the rule would have produced those lines whatever the electorate's politics.

**No representation claim on the stated facts.** A fast-growing suburb that has never elected its preferred candidate is split again, and no lawful, workable comparison with independent roots establishes the real opportunity required by the emerging-electorate route. A merely more favorable map is insufficient. A separate association claim still depends on its own elements.

###### I. Pre-2001 support

* **Retaliation for political participation:** *Elrod v. Burns* (1976), *Branti v. Finkel* (1980), *Rutan v. Republican Party of Illinois* (1990), the simulated *Umbehr*† (1996), and *O'Hare Truck Service v. City of Northlake* (1996).
* **Causation and the same-decision defense:** *Mt. Healthy City School District v. Doyle* (1977), *Arlington Heights* (1977), and the simulated *Brooks*†.
* **A prior arrangement as the benchmark for a loss:** *Beer v. United States* (1976) and the simulated *Bossier*†, statutory and racial, borrowed for method only.
* **Replacement does not automatically moot a challenge:** *City of Mesquite v. Aladdin's Castle* (1982), on voluntary cessation; and the simulated *Northeastern Florida Chapter, Associated General Contractors of America v. City of Jacksonville*†, where the replacement continued the challenged competitive exclusion. Neither establishes that every replacement preserves an injury or automatically revives an injunction. Carrying predecessor election results into the baseline remains this formulation's proposed evidentiary rule, not a holding of either case.
* **Projections and district configurations are relevant evidence:** the *Davis v. Bandemer*\* (1986) plurality, which also holds that one result does not prove durable exclusion.
* **Discipline for comparison maps:** the simulated *Holder*†; *Karcher v. Daggett* (1983).
* **Lawful use of political considerations, and fairness as a legitimate aim:** *Gaffney v. Cummings*\* (1973).
* **The people's choice in congressional elections:** *Wesberry v. Sanders* (1964); the simulated *U.S. Term Limits v. Thornton*† and *Cook v. Gralike*†.
* **Voter approval does not cure an injury:** *Lucas v. Forty-Fourth General Assembly*\* (1964); the simulated *California Democratic Party v. Jones*† and *Romer v. Evans*†.
* **Defeat alone is not injury:** *Whitcomb v. Chavis*\* (1971).

None of the authorities cited here establishes this precise districting test. The application of retaliation doctrine developed here, the foreseeable-range measure, the association branch, the continuity rules, and the even-handedness definition are all proposed.

##### XV. Entrench: governing control beyond the reach of a voting majority

###### A. Scope, right, and constitutional basis

Entrench applies to **an elected governing body chosen from districts**, such as a state legislative chamber, city council, or county commission. **Each chamber is evaluated separately.** It does **not** apply to a State's congressional delegation, which is not a governing body of the State. Congressional claims proceed under Foreclose or other applicable law.

The interest is **electoral reversibility**: the electorate's continuing capacity to replace a governing majority when it withdraws majority support. Entrench contains **no purpose element**.

###### B. What the plaintiff must allege in a complaint

The complaint identifies the body, the map, and the plaintiff's own district. It alleges facts showing that, in recorded conditions where a party or coalition opposing the governing majority holds an absolute majority of the votes for the body, the governing majority keeps control under the challenged map. It may rest on completed elections under the map, on recorded votes re-added under the map, or on both. It also alleges facts indicating that the lines materially cause that result beyond what geographic concentration or other lawful constraints explain. Allegations of uncompetitive districts, an unfair seat share, or probable future advantage do not state the claim.

###### C. Standing

The plaintiff must be assigned to a district whose lines contribute materially to the result, and must show traceability and redressability. A body-wide theory does not give every voter standing.

###### D. Five elements to prove

**Element 1, identifiable governance.** A governing party or coalition exists independently of the lawsuit.

**Element 2, an opposing majority within the foreseeable range.** The recorded range includes conditions in which a party or coherent coalition opposing the governing majority obtains **more than half of the votes for the body**, reliably measured as Section V-A defines. A plurality is not enough.

**Element 3, retention of control in those conditions.** Under the challenged map the governing majority keeps a majority of seats in those conditions. An actual election under the map in which this occurred is the strongest proof. Recorded votes re-added under the map may also establish it.

**Element 4, durability.** The retention holds across the recorded conditions in which the opposition has a majority, and not at one point only. The whole membership of the body must be measured. For a staggered body, an election of part of the seats does not measure the whole. No fixed number of elections or cycles is required, and qualifying elections need not be consecutive.

**Element 5, map causation.** District assignment and placement materially cause the retention beyond what geographic concentration, turnout, candidates, or lawful constraints explain. Those other influences need not be absent; the plaintiff must establish the material contribution of the challenged lines, not sole causation. Comparison maps satisfy the independently grounded lawful-comparison rule in Section IV and, on the recorded votes, would transfer control. The court states that this is an estimate from past returns and addresses the reliability of the inference.

###### E. The State's lawful-constraints answer

The State may show that lawful constraints, consistently applied, made the result unavoidable, including population equality, required Voting Rights Act remedies, applicable contiguity requirements, and real geographic limits. Genuine lawful districting practices receive appropriate weight under Section IV, but a preference for previously established criteria neither excludes another independently grounded lawful comparison nor by itself proves unavoidability. A wish to keep power, approval by referendum, the use of a commission, or another State's conduct is no answer.

###### F. Relief and examples

The court identifies the placements that materially produce the result, and the State redraws first. No seat share, majority, or commission is ordered. Relief before and after an election follows Section III.

**Plaintiff wins on completed elections, on the stated assumptions.** In two full elections under one map, the opposition wins 54 and 51 percent of reliably measured votes for a 110-seat chamber and takes 51 and 47 seats. Independently grounded lawful comparison maps transfer control on the same votes, and the State shows no lawful constraint. Assume reliable evidence also establishes retention of governing control across the full recorded range of opposition-majority conditions, all other elements, and the plaintiff's standing. The two examples alone do not supply those findings or establish a two-election threshold. An opposition candidate's victory in an individual district does not defeat the body-wide injury.

**Plaintiff may win before an election.** A new map is enacted. Re-adding the recorded votes from each election of the prior decade shows that in every year the opposition held a vote majority, the governing party would keep the chamber, while comparison maps would transfer it. The elements can be met if those estimates reliably support retention across the full defined range of opposition-majority conditions and the remaining elements are established. The absence of a completed election under the map is not a merits prerequisite; the court assesses the strength and limitations of the available proof under the ordinary requirements for interim relief.

**State wins on geography.** The same figures arise because the opposition's voters are concentrated in two cities, and comparison maps reproduce the result.

**No claim.** The opposition reaches only a plurality in a three-party field, or the recorded range contains no condition in which it holds a majority.

###### G. Pre-2001 support

* **Justiciability of districting claims:** *Baker v. Carr* (1962); *Davis v. Bandemer*\* (1986).
* **Majority rule and effective representation:** *Reynolds v. Sims* (1964); *Lucas v. Forty-Fourth General Assembly*\* (1964).
* **Coverage of local governing bodies:** *Avery v. Midland County* (1968); *Hadley v. Junior College District* (1970).
* **Limits the operation must respect:** *Whitcomb v. Chavis*\* (1971), that repeated defeat is not itself a denial; *Gaffney v. Cummings*\* (1973), that politics may be considered and proportionality is not required; and the *Bandemer*\* plurality, that one result does not prove durable exclusion.
* **Comparison and remedy:** the simulated *Holder*† and *Growe*†; *White v. Weiser*\* (1973); *Upham v. Seamon*\* (1982).

None of the authorities cited here establishes this proposed entitlement to electoral reversibility. The body-wide control rule, foreseeable-range measure, and absence of a purpose element are all proposed.

##### XVI. The partisan branch's boundaries

| Question | **Foreclose** | **Entrench** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Protected interest | Freedom from one-sided loss of representation or association | Voters can replace a governing majority after withdrawing majority support |
| Scale | Identifiable electorate or association | Entire elected governing body, chamber by chamber |
| Purpose | One-sided purpose required | No purpose element |
| Injury | No capacity to elect across the recorded range, or actual or concretely threatened material impairment of protected political association or activity | Control retained in recorded conditions where the opposition holds a vote majority |
| Election history | Evidence only; no fixed number; provable before an election | Evidence only; no fixed number; an actual inversion is the strongest proof |
| Congressional districts | Yes | No |
| State's main answer | Same reassignment without the one-sided purpose; even-handed fairness allowed | Lawful constraints or geography |
| Court's order | Remove the proved operation; no guaranteed seat | Remove the causal placements; no guaranteed seats or control |

Political speech, party advocacy, direct ballot access, and election administration continue to be governed by their own constitutional protections. **Foreclose and Entrench do not exhaust election law.**

---

#### Part IV. Overlap, Complaints, and Administration

##### XVII. Direct partisan Exclude, Mark, and Burden

The racial five and partisan two are not a complete catalogue of every possible election wrong. **Exclude, Mark, and Burden describe functions that can also be committed against political parties, candidates, and their supporters.** Their *facts* may look similar to the racial operations, but the constitutional **standard follows the characteristic, the right, and the state power at issue**.

For example, a State may make a party's candidate satisfy materially harsher filing requirements than the other party's candidates (**partisan Burden**), exclude an otherwise qualifying candidate based on a protected political position (**partisan Exclude**), or place an official adverse policy-compliance statement beside a congressional candidate's name (**partisan Mark**). These injuries can be complete when a presently operative rule is imposed, without waiting for a completed election. They **do not** inherit the racial categorical prohibition merely from having the same functional name.

Real *Anderson v. Celebrezze* (1983) and the simulated *Burdick v. Takushi*† (1992) provide the familiar ballot-regulation balancing approach: the court assesses the **character and severity of the burden** against the State's **actual interests and justification**, with severe burdens needing correspondingly strong justification and ordinary, neutral administration ordinarily enjoying latitude. Other constitutional constraints may be more direct. The simulated *Cook v. Gralike* prohibits using the **official congressional ballot** to disadvantage candidates for refusing the State's desired position on federal legislation; the Elections Clause limits state power to impose that official policy-compliance penalty. *Cook* is not an automatic prohibition on every truthful, neutral election notice, and *Anderson/Burdick* should not be treated as overriding the source-specific congressional ballot rule.

**Thus:** a racial Mark as narrowly defined in Part II is categorically forbidden under this proposed test; a political-policy Mark must be evaluated under the applicable First Amendment, Article I, and electoral-burden rules. A racial Exclude can immediately violate the franchise; a neutral candidate-filing deadline that incidentally disadvantages a political group may survive the governing burden-and-interest analysis. The shared *operation* vocabulary is descriptive, not a transfer of the racial liability standard.

**Pre-2001 support for the direct partisan operations:** *Williams v. Rhodes*\* (1968), on ballot rules that shut out competitors; *Anderson v. Celebrezze* (1983); *Tashjian v. Republican Party of Connecticut* (1986) and *Eu v. San Francisco County Democratic Central Committee* (1989), on associational rights of parties; and the simulated *Norman v. Reed*†, *Burdick*†, *Timmons*†, *Thornton*†, *Jones*†, and *Cook*†.

##### XVIII. When the same map is both racial and partisan

1. **Separate pleadings and findings.** The plaintiff may assert racial Dilute, proposed racial Segregate, partisan Foreclose, and, where the body and history qualify, partisan Entrench on overlapping facts. Each count identifies its own interest, parties with standing, required purpose, necessary electoral effect, constitutional source, and applicable State answer. A race claim does not automatically establish a party claim, and a party claim does not automatically establish a race claim.
2. **Partisan explanations do not erase racial wrongs.** If a State says it split a Black community because it voted for the opposing party, the court treats that reason as evidence of legislative choice. It still asks whether race itself was a substantial or motivating factor and whether racial opportunity was actually harmed. Simulated *Easley* bars a partisan label from defeating an **already proved** racial wrong.
3. **Racial proof does not automatically establish Foreclose.** The political explanation may help identify motive. The representation branch additionally requires a demonstrated opportunity or the express emerging-electorate alternative, and inability to elect across the defined recorded range. The association branch requires protected political association or activity and actual or concretely threatened material impairment shown under ordinary proof rules, without inability to elect or nominate or a freestanding durability requirement. Either branch also requires prohibited **one-sided targeting** and map causation. Those facts cannot be inferred from racial intent alone.
4. **A lawful partisan fairness policy may be a Foreclose defense, not an escape from proved racial discrimination.** An established and evenly applied fairness plan can show the State would have made the same partisan reassignment absent punitive targeting. It cannot by its label excuse a racial Exclude, Mark, or other fully proved race-specific violation. A race claim receives its own causation and justification analysis.
5. **Statutory claims remain independent.** Section 2 and other applicable election statutes keep their own definitions of harm, liability, voting opportunity, and remedy. A statutory violation can require a district to be changed even when no constitutional Foreclose, Entrench, or racial design-only claim succeeds.
6. **One map, one proportionate cure.** When multiple counts are established, the remedial order lists each violation and the line changes or practices necessary to cure them. One adequate legislative redraw can satisfy several wrongs. The judge never orders duplicate, incompatible maps.

<a id="test-forms"></a>

##### XIX. Complaint drafting: what must appear on the page

This section illustrates how to plead the operation-specific elements. The forms below contain **illustrative fictional allegations**, not filed or verified historical complaints. Their use does not establish the sufficiency of an actual pleading. Bracketed references are placeholders for supported facts. A plaintiff should plead the elements for the chosen operation, not paste all seven theories into every case.

###### A. Common opening allegations

Identify:

1. The defendant State or election official.
2. The rule, map, district, or procedure being challenged.
3. How the plaintiff is personally subjected to it.
4. The election or governing body affected.
5. The precise civic interest.
6. Specific supporting facts.
7. Relief that would redress the plaintiff's position.

Do **not** merely say that the map is partisan or racial, or that the plaintiff is a voter in the State.

**Example standing allegation**

> Plaintiff resides and votes in District 6 as assigned by the challenged enactment. The enacted boundaries divide Plaintiff's precinct from the neighboring precincts identified in paragraphs 18 through 24, and Plaintiff's own political participation occurs through that assignment. The requested alteration of the specified boundaries would change the arrangement to which Plaintiff is subject.

**Application note.** That alleges personal exposure and redressability; the plaintiff must still prove them if disputed.

###### B. Illustrative racial counts

**Count I — Exclude**

> Before the amendment, Plaintiff was an eligible voter in the City election. The boundary enactment removed her residence and comparable Black neighborhoods from the City electorate while retaining adjacent white neighborhoods. Drafting instructions and the selection of neighborhoods show the boundary used race as an operative criterion. Plaintiff seeks invalidation of the exclusion and restoration of lawful municipal voting eligibility.

**Count II — Mark**

> The State's official ballot places a government-supplied racial label beside Plaintiff's name as a candidate, and does the same to candidates of other races. Plaintiff challenges the State's official insertion of race as part of the voters' choice. She seeks removal of the official racial designation, not a change in voting results.

**Count III — Burden**

> In the challenged precincts, registration applicants including Plaintiff must supply two additional documents that other applicants need not supply. County instructions and actual practices tie the additional requirement to the racial composition of those precincts. The extra condition materially restricts access to ordinary voter registration. Plaintiff seeks the ordinary procedure.

**Application note.** A structural *Wilson* count instead states the different processes required to pursue racial-remedial policies and comparable ordinary policies.

**Count III-B — Burden through racial fragmentation of participation, proposed application**

> Plaintiff participates in the identified neighborhood coalition, whose meetings, nomination work, and concrete plans to organize are described in paragraphs [x] through [y]. The challenged assignments divide that activity through the specific mechanism described in paragraph [z]. The division materially obstructs the coalition's actual participation or realistic coalition-building opportunity, as shown by [supported facts]. The identified drafting records and surrounding circumstances show that the State selected the assignments because of that racial impairment. Plaintiff seeks correction of the causal assignments or practices, without a guaranteed seat, racial percentage, or election outcome.

**Application note.** A plaintiff relying on concrete efforts to begin participation should identify those efforts rather than invent an existing organization. Prove material impairment, racial purpose or operative treatment, and causation under Section IX; a preferred boundary, abstract influence, or racial composition alone is insufficient. The State retains the defense applicable to the claim.


**Count IV — Dilute**

> The enacted plan divides the identified cohesive Black electorate among three districts. Drafting records show the division was selected in substantial part to weaken its ability to elect its preferred candidates, and voting records and realistic geographically anchored comparisons show material loss of effective electoral opportunity. Plaintiff challenges the intentional impairment, not the map's shape or the absence of any racial percentage.

**Count V — Segregate, proposed**

> Across the four districts of the identified municipality, the legislature coordinated boundaries to put the two neighboring racial communities into separate political constituencies. Drafting directives show separation itself was a substantial objective, and the enacted map substantially carries it out. Plaintiff is assigned to the partition and seeks removal of this organized racial separation, without demanding a district of any racial composition.

**Application note.** This remains **a request to establish a new simulated doctrine**, not a count automatically controlled by existing *Miller*.

###### C. Illustrative partisan counts

**Count VI — Foreclose, representation branch, proposed**

> Under the prior lawful map, voters in the identified District 8 precincts, including Plaintiff, elected their preferred candidates in each of the elections listed in paragraph [x]. The new plan divides those precincts among three districts. Re-adding the recorded votes from each of those elections under the new lines shows that the electorate's candidate would lose in all three districts in every one. The facts and inference detailed in paragraph [y] support inability across the full recorded range. Legislative materials show the reassignment was substantially intended to penalize this electorate for its voting history and to benefit its opponents. Independently grounded lawful comparison maps preserve the opportunity. Plaintiff seeks a limited redraw.

**Application note.** Under the express emerging-electorate alternative, the count substitutes concrete facts about the identified electorate's cohesion and the real opportunity established through independently grounded lawful comparisons for the allegation of previously exercised opportunity; all remaining elements still apply. A count under the association branch instead identifies the actual association or concrete ongoing or ready-to-undertake associational activity, the mechanism of obstruction, actual or concretely threatened material impairment, prohibited one-sided purpose, and causation. It requires neither a formal organization nor inability to elect or nominate nor a freestanding durability showing.

**Count VI-B — Foreclose, association branch, proposed**

> Plaintiff participates in the actual political association identified in paragraphs [x] through [y], or has concrete ongoing or ready-to-undertake efforts to form or participate in that association as described there. The new district assignments obstruct that activity through [the specific administrative, organizational, nomination, or participation mechanism], producing or concretely threatening the material impairment described in paragraph [z]. The enactment records and comparative treatment show that the State selected the mechanism substantially to penalize this electorate for its political participation and benefit its opponents. The asserted legitimate considerations would not have produced the same obstruction, as the identified lawful evidence and comparisons show. Plaintiff seeks removal of the causal impediment.

**Application note.** Plead the specific facts supporting purpose, impairment, and causation; do not merely copy these conclusions. Formal incorporation, inability to nominate or win, an election defeat, and a separate duration showing are unnecessary. A serious temporary obstruction at a critical election stage can qualify. The State's same-decision defense remains governed by Section XIV, and the plaintiff's allegation about legitimate considerations does not transfer that defense's burden.

**Count VII — Entrench, proposed**

> Plaintiff resides in District 12 of the elected State Assembly. In each recorded election identified in paragraph [x], the independently identifiable opposition party won an absolute majority of the reliably measured Assembly votes. Under the challenged plan the governing party retains a majority of seats in each of those conditions, as shown by [the completed elections under the plan] [the recorded votes re-added under the plan]. The facts and inference detailed in paragraph [y] support retention across the full recorded range of opposition-majority conditions. The specific packing and splitting detailed in paragraphs [z] through [aa], including Plaintiff's district, materially cause that result. Independently grounded lawful comparison maps would transfer control on the same votes. Plaintiff seeks correction of the causal districting arrangement, not any party seat share.

**Application note.** The vote totals and the treatment of uncontested seats must be supported with concrete allegations and ultimately trial evidence.

**Direct partisan Mark or Burden**

> The State puts an adverse official policy-compliance label beside Plaintiff's congressional candidacy, based on refusal to take a prescribed position on federal legislation.

**Alternative illustrative allegation:**

> The State imposes a materially higher nomination threshold only on candidates of the disfavored party.

**Application note.** The claim then explains the **applicable First Amendment, Article I, or electoral-access standard**, rather than treating the racial Mark's categorical rule as transferable.

###### D. What no complaint can substitute for the elements

The following assertions, standing alone, are insufficient: a legislator said race predominated; the majority-minority district is unusually narrow; the opponent has an unfairly safe seat; statewide party seat share differs from total votes; a famous incumbent lost; the new map was drawn mid-decade; a party controlled the mapmaking commission; a majority of the public voted for the map; or an expert can imagine a more competitive arrangement. Each can be evidence of something else. None identifies a prohibited operation by itself.

There are also **no universal prerequisites** of financial damages, a second governmental action, an expert report with the complaint, an ideal substitute map, a candidate defeat, or a predetermined numeric racial threshold. **Operation-specific requirements remain mandatory**. Foreclose's representation loss across the recorded range, its distinct material association impairment, and Entrench's control retained across the opposition-majority conditions cannot be pleaded away by invoking the universal rule.

##### XX. Case-management instructions for trial courts

The judge should enter a claim-specific schedule and make separate findings, in approximately this order:

1. **Separate claims.** Identify the State act, constitutional or statutory source, specific racial or partisan operation, and the requested cure. A Section 2 count is not reformulated as a civic-deprivation count.
2. **Resolve personal standing.** Determine who is actually subject to each challenged assignment, designation, participation restriction, or electoral system. A plaintiff may have standing for one count and not another.
3. **Test the legal adequacy of the complaint.** Dismiss a motive- or composition-only count. Allow appropriately pleaded disputed facts about an actual operation to proceed under ordinary procedural rules.
4. **Require the right electoral record.** For racial Mark or Exclude, enactment and application can establish the injury. For racial Burden, assess comparable access. For racial Dilute, evaluate racial purpose and material opportunity impairment. For proposed Segregate, assess separation purpose and coordinated actual partition. For Foreclose, require either a demonstrated opportunity or the emerging-electorate alternative, together with representation loss across the recorded range; or require protected political association or activity and its independently shown actual or concretely threatened material impairment, without inability to elect or nominate or a freestanding durability requirement. For Entrench, require reliable body-wide vote evidence and proof that control is retained in the recorded conditions where the opposition holds a majority.
5. **State the precise causal findings.** Do not treat a map shape, race or party percentage, or a comparison map as a substitute for injury. Explain whether population, residential geography, actual voting, legal constraints, or the challenged government choice produced the harm.
6. **Apply the branch-specific State answer.** Use categorical racial rules only where they apply; simulated *Adarand* only where the racial classification rule calls for it; same-decision defenses only for the relevant purpose claims; *Anderson/Burdick* or Article I for direct political ballot burdens as appropriate; lawful-constraint causation for Entrench.
7. **Order one fitted cure.** Identify the invalid provision or affected districts; permit the State a meaningful first remedy; supervise only what is necessary; draw judicial lines only when the authorized and necessary final option exists.

###### Seven outcome controls

**Racial concentration only:** State wins the race-based constitutional district-design count despite proven predominance, because no prohibited operation is identified. An independent Voting Rights Act claim remains possible.

**Racial franchise exclusion:** the personally excluded voter wins on a proved operative racial electorate restriction, regardless of whether another citizen's ballot is unchanged.

**Official racial ballot label:** the designated candidate wins the proposed categorical Mark claim even if the ballot labels every race identically.

**Deliberate regional separation:** the plaintiff may prevail under Segregate **if the Court adopts that new category** and the record shows the separation objective, a coordinated regional partition, and actual implementation, on the suit of a plaintiff with standing; demographics alone fail.

**Aggressive political map before an election:** Foreclose's representation branch or Entrench may be established if reliable evidence supports their respective electoral injuries across the defined range. Foreclose's association branch instead requires ordinary proof of actual or concretely threatened material impairment of protected political association or activity; it has no separate durability or inability-to-win threshold. A sponsor's goal or unsupported forecast is insufficient; reliable inference from the record is permitted. Interim relief requires its own findings.

**One-sided loss of capacity:** Foreclose succeeds if a demonstrated opportunity, or the opportunity established through the express emerging-electorate alternative, has been lost across the recorded range for the prohibited purpose, the remaining elements are proved, and the State cannot prove the same-decision defense. An electorate that would still win in any destination district under some condition within that range has no representation claim; its distinct association claim remains subject to that branch's elements.

**Control retained against a voting majority:** Entrench may succeed if reliable body-wide data and independently grounded lawful comparison maps prove that the lines materially keep the governing party in control across the recorded conditions where its opponents hold a majority. If lawful geographic constraints explain the retention without a material causal contribution from the challenged placements, the map-causation claim fails. A seat-vote mismatch without the required proof is insufficient.

---

##### Final proposed controlling formulation

> **The Independent Civic Deprivation Test.** When a plaintiff challenges the State's consideration of race or political affiliation in election administration or district design, the constitutional question is not whether the characteristic predominated but whether the State made it perform a prohibited operation on a protected incident of the plaintiff's political membership. A design-only complaint states no violation. A court first resolves personal standing, then identifies and adjudicates the pleaded operation under its own elements, proof, causation, justification, and remedy rules. In racial claims, the operations are Exclude, Mark, Burden, Dilute, and the proposed and as-yet-unadopted Segregate. In partisan districting claims, the proposed operations are Foreclose and Entrench. Foreclose's representation branch measures inability to elect in any destination district across the defined recorded range, subject to its express emerging-electorate alternative; its association branch protects an actual political association or concrete ongoing or ready-to-undertake efforts to form or participate in one against actual or concretely threatened material impairment. Formal organization, inability to elect or nominate, a freestanding durability showing, and an electoral-range prerequisite are unnecessary for that branch. Entrench measures retention of governing control across the recorded opposition-majority conditions. Each branch remains subject to its own elements and defenses. Direct partisan exclusion, marking, and access burdens remain governed by the constitutional standards applicable to those acts, not by the race-specific categorical rules. Proven racial and partisan wrongs may overlap; relief is a single necessary cure, with the State first permitted to remedy unlawful district lines. Independent Voting Rights Act rights and remedies remain unaffected.

###### Authorities and limits on their use

**Simulation sources and limits:** the simulated holdings register and the equality-and-voting foundation, including Foundation 15. No independent verification of a separate standards register is claimed. Every extension expressly labeled **proposed** requires adoption in a simulated opinion before it can be cited as a holding.

**Selected historical primary sources:** [*Gaffney v. Cummings*](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/412/735), [*Anderson v. Celebrezze*](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/460/780/), [*Burdick v. Takushi*](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/504/428), [*Cook v. Gralike*](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/531/510/), and [*Davis v. Bandemer*](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/478/109/). These support the identified historical propositions; none is cited as having already held that Foreclose or Entrench is governing law.

---

##### E. What no authority in sections A through C supplies

The following are proposed and would be new law on adoption: the unified pleading rule as a stated rule; the categorical rule for Mark; the extension of Exclude to candidacy; the coalition and association form of Burden; the no-separate-justification rule for intentional Dilute and purposeful Burden; Segregate in full, which is reserved; Foreclose in full, including its two branches, the protective association refinement without a separate durability threshold, the foreseeable-range measure, the continuity rules, the independently grounded comparison route for emerging electorates, the present-membership standing formulation, and the even-handedness definition; Entrench in full, including electoral reversibility and the foreseeable-range measure; the independently grounded lawful-comparison rule in Section IV; and the standard-of-proof, accrual, and reassessment rules in Section III.

<a id="complete-docket"></a>

### 6.2 Complete docket plan — October Terms 2001 through 2026

Prepared November 10, 2026. This plan retains all 65 voting, districting, participation, and related enforcement entries across all 26 terms. OT2010 has an explicit gap rather than an invented case. An October Term begins in October of its named year; a June decision ordinarily belongs to the preceding year's term. A lower-court comparison, hypothetical grant, reconstructed appeal, pending matter, and actual Supreme Court decision are identified separately.

Each entry states the question, Stone's recommended procedural or merits disposition, its legal basis, and what the opinion must announce, explain, define, or reserve. A missing appellate record produces a concrete next step rather than fabricated findings. Background matters receive a recommended use, not an invented Supreme Court vote. References to a proposed earlier decision are conditional on its actual adoption in this timeline.

#### Legal foundation and sequence

**Existing fictional holdings control the simulation.** The proposed rules below require adoption before they govern later cases. Historical opinions supply facts, legal comparisons, and competing arguments; they do not supply fictional votes. A preferred result must still follow from a properly presented claim, applicable law, and the actual record.

| Existing fictional authority | Rule carried into this docket |
|---|---|
| Shaw, Miller, Vera, Easley | Racial consideration in a shared territorial district is not itself the injury. Preserve independently established exclusion, dilution, unequal participation, and inferior civic status. Partisanship does not excuse a proved racial wrong. |
| Gingles line, De Grandy, Holder, Valdespino | Keep statutory opportunity, workable comparison, and totality distinct. Dependable crossover support may satisfy the fictional opportunity rule; mere influence and proportional-seat arithmetic do not. |
| Bossier I and II | Separate §5 retrogression from its purpose inquiry; the submitting jurisdiction carries the preclearance burden. Do not automatically transplant the constitutional same-decision defense into §5. |
| Brooks | Attach prohibited purpose to the operative enactment. After a motivating-purpose showing, assess the distinct same-enactment defense and its burden. |
| Burdick and Reform Party | Evaluate actual election-access burdens and their justifications; districting thresholds do not govern every election rule. |
| Growe, Lopez, Sinkfield, Chen | Respect timely lawful state remedies, require personal injury and a proper procedural vehicle, and distinguish representational population from electoral-opportunity measures. |
| Boerne | Preventive Fourteenth Amendment enforcement must fit constitutional injuries. The completed fictional decision reserves the separate Fifteenth Amendment question. |

**Proposed development order.** *Cano* addresses discriminatory purpose and the scope of concrete racial participation injury; *Arakaki* addresses ancestry-based candidacy restrictions. *Vieth* selects a bounded partisan standard, with *Martinez* held for it. *Larios* separately enforces population equality. *LULAC* applies statutory opportunity and the selected partisan rule. *Bartlett* applies the existing fictional crossover holding. *Shelby County* addresses preventive enforcement. *Alabama* distinguishes preserved racial participation and dilution claims while reserving Segregate. *Gill* resolves standing before any merits application. *Lamone*, *Rucho*, and Michigan then test the chosen partisan standard on developed records.

No case announces every operation merely because it is a convenient place to collect rules. A remand must concern a preserved claim or a procedurally available amendment; it does not automatically create a fresh claim after a failed trial. No hypothetical majority, colleague’s vote, or opinion assignment is supplied.

**History convention.** The docket uses actual records as proposed vehicles even where earlier fictional outcomes would have changed the litigation. In particular, retaining preclearance after *Shelby County*, rejecting racial-design liability, and deciding *Callais* differently would change later maps and procedural paths. The affected entries identify that reconstruction rather than present the real sequence as inevitable.

#### October Term index

[OT2001](#october-term-2001) · [OT2002](#october-term-2002) · [OT2003](#october-term-2003) · [OT2004](#october-term-2004) · [OT2005](#october-term-2005) · [OT2006](#october-term-2006) · [OT2007](#october-term-2007) · [OT2008](#october-term-2008) · [OT2009](#october-term-2009) · [OT2010](#october-term-2010) · [OT2011](#october-term-2011) · [OT2012](#october-term-2012) · [OT2013](#october-term-2013) · [OT2014](#october-term-2014) · [OT2015](#october-term-2015) · [OT2016](#october-term-2016) · [OT2017](#october-term-2017) · [OT2018](#october-term-2018) · [OT2019](#october-term-2019) · [OT2020](#october-term-2020) · [OT2021](#october-term-2021) · [OT2022](#october-term-2022) · [OT2023](#october-term-2023) · [OT2024](#october-term-2024) · [OT2025](#october-term-2025) · [OT2026](#october-term-2026)

#### October Term 2001

##### Page v. Bartels — preserving effective crossover opportunity

**History and docket placement.** The three-judge District of New Jersey tried the challenge to New Jersey's legislative map April 30–May 1, 2001, entered judgment May 2, and published its opinion May 7, 144 F. Supp. 2d 346. OT2001 merits review is a proposed reconstruction: *Robertson* confirms that an appeal was filed, but its ultimate disposition remains unverified. The earlier Third Circuit reversal concerned the required three-judge court, not the completed merits judgment. [District opinion](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/district-courts/FSupp2/144/346/2315965/); [Federal Judicial Center history](https://www.fjc.gov/sites/default/files/materials/13/EE-NJ-2-01-cv-1733-Page.pdf); [Robertson, footnote 3](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/district-courts/FSupp2/148/443/2567884/).

**Questions.** Did reducing Black voting-age population in District 27 from 52.8% to 27.5%, while increasing it in District 34 from 3.9% to 35.3%, deny equal electoral opportunity? Did the changes intentionally impair voting or political participation despite the trial court's contrary findings?

**Stone’s recommended disposition.** Affirm denial of injunctive relief on the findings of preserved effective opportunity and insufficient proof of the alleged constitutional injuries. No replacement map follows.

**Reasoning.** The decisive statutory finding was failure to establish the third *Gingles* condition: relevant opposing bloc voting did not ordinarily defeat the affected voters' preferred candidates. The court credited district-specific electoral projections, crossover support, and primary-participation evidence for both districts, even without counting Hispanic support. Simulated *Valdespino* permits dependable crossover capacity below fifty percent; *De Grandy* rejects maximization. Neither racial percentages nor candidates' race substitutes for identifying voters' choices and actual opportunity. The credited record also failed to establish intentional dilution or the predicted participation chilling. [Gingles](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/478/30/); [De Grandy](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/512/997/).

**What the opinion must articulate.** Apply existing law rather than announce a numerical safe harbor. Opportunity must remain for the affected voters; gains elsewhere cannot compensate for a proved local deprivation. Counting demonstrated Hispanic crossover support does not decide whether distinct protected groups may combine into one statutory class.
