**Reasoning.** The proposed reconciliation keeps *Miller*’s attention to personal exposure while separating standing from the merits rule rejecting racial design as an independent violation. A plaintiff must connect the challenged governmental action to her own cognizable injury and requested relief. The earlier proposal would abandon Stone’s narrower *Vera* approach to the extent it categorically forecloses that connection.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Identify the actual plaintiffs’ injury, traceability and redressability; explain any change from Stone’s *Vera* writing; and reserve the merits elements not necessary to the standing decision. Do not treat an allegation as a proved dilution injury.

##### Bush v. Palm Beach County Canvassing Board — clarification of state law

**History and docket placement.** The tracker places the December 4, 2000 matter on the OT2000 list, citing 531 U.S. 70, No. 00-836.

**Questions.** Did the Florida Supreme Court alter the legislature’s pre-election selection rules, or interpret those rules? What does the federal safe-harbor provision require, and what consequences follow from failing to obtain its protection?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Join a narrow vacatur and remand for clarification of the basis of the Florida Supreme Court’s judgment.

**Reasoning.** Ordinary judicial interpretation of state election statutes does not itself replace the legislature’s method of appointing presidential electors. Clarification should identify the state-law basis before deciding the Article II issue. The safe harbor supplies a legal advantage when its conditions are met, rather than a general command to terminate every unresolved contest on that date.

**What the opinion must articulate.** State the federal issue requiring clarification and the limited scope of remand. Distinguish loss of safe-harbor protection from a prohibition on continued counting or adjudication.

##### Bush v. Gore — consistent counting and the source of a remedial standard

**History and docket placement.** The older tracker lists the December 12, 2000 matter, 531 U.S. 98, No. 00-949, as open. That status must be checked against any later completed fictional decision before this plan is used.

**Questions.** Does the recount treat materially equivalent ballots inconsistently? Does the state court implement pre-election law or replace the legislature’s rules? Can a post-election operational standard lawfully implement a pre-election voter-intent rule?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Dissolve the stay; vacate only the procedures allowing materially equivalent ballots to receive inconsistent treatment; reject the Article II challenge under the proposed limited federal-review rule; and remand for continued counting under a uniform statewide standard implementing Florida’s pre-election law. Declare no winner.

**Reasoning.** Equal treatment concerns the method for evaluating comparable ballots. The proposed Article II inquiry asks whether the state court materially replaced the legislature’s pre-election rules or reassigned authority, rather than treating every state-law interpretation as a federal violation. December 12 confers a safe-harbor advantage; the older plan does not treat it as an absolute stop date, and leaves unresolved electoral-count matters to the legally assigned congressional process.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Explain why the remedial operational standard implements existing law rather than changes it. Address the tracker’s tension between standards communicated before counting and a post-election clarification. Identify lawful authority for the remedy, the ballots to which it applies and the constitutional limits on federal intervention.

##### Easley v. Cromartie — reconstruct the preserved claim first

**History and docket placement.** The tracker lists April 18, 2001, 532 U.S. 234, Nos. 99-1864 and 99-1865. Its original deletion instruction was superseded by the fictional *Hunt v. Cromartie* decision and the OT2000 listing. The historical predominance dispute requires a reconstructed posture in this timeline.

**Questions.** What independent claim survived *Hunt*’s mandate? Is the asserted injury intentional packing that impairs electoral opportunity, or only racial predominance? Do official descriptions of a district have legal operation, or bear only on the proof of purpose?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Reconstruct the posture before deciding the case. If no independent claim was preserved, give judgment for the State under *Hunt*. If an appropriately reconstructed, preserved packing-based dilution claim exists, adjudicate that claim under its own elements rather than the abandoned design-only rule.

**Reasoning.** The fictional *Hunt* rule makes racial predominance insufficient for liability. That rule does not excuse a separately established deprivation simply because the government invokes partisan aims. A hypothetical reconstruction must identify the surviving claim and actual factual dispute instead of treating historical predominance findings as proof of dilution.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Specify the reconstruction and preservation basis, distinguish evidence of motive from the asserted injury, and identify any factual questions requiring further proceedings. Do not manufacture a preserved claim from an unsuccessful design-only action.

##### Brentwood Academy v. Tennessee Secondary School Athletic Association — state action

**History and docket placement.** Carried forward from the tracker’s OT2000 list.

**Questions.** Does the association’s actual governance and institutional relationship with public schools make its challenged conduct state action? What remains to be decided on the substantive claim?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Reverse the categorical private-actor ruling and remand the merits.

**Reasoning.** Actual institutional governance and entwinement can establish state action. Membership, public funding or regulation alone does not invariably establish it. The earlier plan treats this analysis as complementary to *Lebron* and *American Manufacturers*.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Identify the governmental participation that supplies attribution, distinguish it from ordinary funding or regulation, and reserve the unresolved merits rather than treating state action as liability.

##### Cook v. Gralike — official ballot penalties

**History and docket placement.** Carried forward from the tracker’s OT2000 list; linked to its question F2.

**Questions.** May a State place adverse official labels beside congressional candidates who decline to support a prescribed term-limits policy? Does the Elections Clause authorize that use of the ballot?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Affirm invalidation of Missouri’s adverse ballot labels.

**Reasoning.** Authority to regulate the manner of congressional elections does not authorize the State to favor a prescribed policy commitment or handicap particular federal candidates through its official ballot. The earlier plan extends the institutional reasoning of *Term Limits* to this device.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Distinguish election administration from an official electoral penalty. State the Article I basis and avoid importing the proposed categorical rule for racial ballot designations into every partisan or policy-related label.

##### Alexander v. Sandoval — private enforcement of implementing regulations

**History and docket placement.** Carried forward from the tracker’s OT2000 list; linked to question P1. The older proposal conflicts with *Powell v. Ridge*, which Stone joined.

**Questions.** Can the existing private action enforce prohibited conduct specified by an authorized Title VI implementing regulation? What change to the fictional *Powell* rule would that require?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Affirm the private enforcement action within the granted question, following the legal approach of the historical dissent. This remains a proposal requiring an express treatment of contrary fictional precedent.

**Reasoning.** An authorized implementing regulation may specify conduct reached by the statutory scheme, and the earlier plan would permit the existing private action to reach it. Section 601 retains its intent requirement; the regulatory effects inquiry retains its own justification and alternatives requirements. The plan cannot be described as consistent with *Powell* without identifying a legally sufficient distinction or an express change.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Identify the source of the private action, the authority and scope of the regulation, and the precise treatment of *Powell*. Distinguish this implied-action issue from the legislatively supplied §1983 remedy considered in *Gonzaga*.

##### Buckhannon Board & Care Home v. West Virginia Department of Health and Human Resources — catalyst fees

**History and docket placement.** Carried forward from the tracker’s OT2000 list; linked to question P3.

**Questions.** Can a plaintiff qualify for statutory fees when a legally grounded lawsuit materially causes the defendant to supply the requested relief without a judicial merits judgment? What proof separates causation from mere sequence?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Reverse the categorical rejection of catalyst fees and remand, following the historical dissent’s approach.

**Reasoning.** The older plan requires a nonfrivolous, legally grounded suit that materially caused the relief. Filing before an unrelated voluntary policy change does not by itself satisfy that requirement.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Ground the fee rule in the applicable statute and explain the causation showing. A remand should require the necessary findings rather than assume entitlement to fees from the defendant’s change alone.

##### Brewer v. West Irondequoit Central School District — conditional plan for a vehicle marked for deletion

**History and docket placement.** The tracker records a decision to delete this OT2000 vehicle to preserve the later school-assignment question, but says the deletion had not yet been applied. The following merits proposal is therefore retained only as an older conditional plan, not an instruction to restore the case.

**Questions.** If the vehicle remains, does the preliminary injunction rest on a categorical rejection of reducing de facto racial isolation as a lawful objective? Do the program’s particular eligibility rules and operation satisfy the applicable standard?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** If the case is retained, vacate the mandatory preliminary injunction admitting the white student to the extent it rests on the categorical premise, and remand for a developed merits record under fictional *Eisenberg*, with prompt reconsideration of interim relief.

**Reasoning.** An impermissibly categorical rejection of the integration objective does not resolve the validity of the actual program. Its rigid racial eligibility rules and withdrawal of the child’s admission after her race became known raise distinct questions about the fit between the objective, means and burdens.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Resolve the vehicle’s deletion status before using the merits plan. Verify the judgment actually under review: the tracker’s question R5 says the historical Second Circuit had already vacated the preliminary injunction, which may require a different appellate disposition from the older plan. If reached, identify current legal authority and the actual operation of the program; any interim order must address the child’s present school year. Preserve the reserved assignment-method question to the extent the case does not require its resolution.

##### Sterling v. Borough of Minersville — conditional privacy and immunity plan

**History and docket placement.** The tracker records an OT2000 vehicle whose deletion had been decided but not applied. Its question O4 retains an older approach if the case remains; that approach does not restore a deleted vehicle.

**Questions.** Does informational privacy protect an adult arrestee against an officer’s threat to reveal suspected sexual orientation to a family member? Was the right clearly established in April 1997? Does the privacy doctrine cover a threat or disclosure to one relative, and is any independent equality claim preserved?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** If the case is retained, resolve the presented privacy and conduct-date qualified-immunity issues without adopting a constitutional scrutiny tier for sexual orientation. The older tracker supplies no firm affirmance, reversal or damages disposition on those unresolved questions.

**Reasoning.** The privacy injury and the official’s immunity require their own legal inquiries. The tracker identifies *Sheets* as protecting objectively intimate information against intentional official disclosure causing public dissemination, while cautioning that *Doe v. SEPTA* did not declare dissemination indispensable. Those descriptions frame the unresolved analogy; they do not themselves decide whether the alleged threat is actionable or whether the relevant law was clearly established.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Verify the vehicle’s status, identify the precise protected information and challenged act, and assess immunity at the conduct date. Separate a privacy ruling from any preserved equality claim and distinguish Stone’s personal scrutiny preference from a holding required by this case.

##### Chen v. City of Houston — representational population

**History and docket placement.** The tracker retained this OT2000 vehicle despite its overlap with the later *Evenwel* question. It links the matter to questions D12 and D16.

**Questions.** May a jurisdiction equalize total population when drawing districts? Does the separate racial claim allege a cognizable deprivation beyond district design?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Affirm the judgment for Houston on total population. Reject the design-only racial claim unless the preserved pleadings identify independent dilution, exclusion, unequal participation or another cognizable injury requiring decision.

**Reasoning.** Representatives serve residents, including children and people who cannot presently vote. Electoral eligibility and representational population therefore perform different functions. The fictional racial-districting rule separately rejects design alone as a constitutional injury.

**What the opinion must articulate.** State why total population is permissible without automatically resolving every alternative denominator. Identify the preserved racial claim and distinguish its disposition from the population holding; explain the effect on the later *Evenwel* question only as a consequence of the actual holding adopted.

##### Tuan Anh Nguyen v. INS — the father-only condition and the remedy

**History and docket placement.** The tracker lists June 11, 2001, 533 U.S. 53, No. 99-2071, and intends to resolve the remedy as well as liability. The earlier remedial proposal conflicts with Stone’s recorded *Miller v. Albright* dissent.

**Questions.** Does §1409(a)(4)’s father-only condition satisfy the applicable heightened scrutiny? If it fails, does lawful relief require severance and recognition of citizenship, an equal proof regime, time for Congress, or another remedy supported by the statute?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** The older plan would invalidate the condition, sever it under the INA’s severability clause, recognize citizenship at birth under the remaining statute, and vacate the removal order. **The direction of relief remains unresolved:** Stone’s *Miller* dissent instead favored a remand to determine a lawful remedy, with citizenship recognition only if no narrower cure sufficed.

**Reasoning.** The liability proposal applies heightened scrutiny to the sex-based condition. The remedial consequence requires a separate analysis of the remaining statute, severability and lawful judicial authority; a finding of unequal treatment does not alone supply the particular benefit.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Explain the scrutiny analysis and expressly resolve the inconsistency with Stone’s earlier remedial writing. State whether the Court extends, withdraws or restructures the condition and why that result follows from the statute. The later *Morales-Santana* matter becomes an application only to the extent this fictional decision actually answers its question.

#### October Term 2001 — other carried-forward plans

##### Adarand Constructors v. Mineta — the dispute actually presented

**History and docket placement.** Carried forward from the tracker’s OT2001 list; linked to question R4.

**Questions.** Does a judgment concerning state and local federal-aid procurement properly present a challenge to direct federal procurement? Do standing and the granted issues permit a merits decision?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Dismiss the writ as improvidently granted if the historical mismatch remains. If a proper merits dispute is presented, apply the controlling standard rather than force dismissal to reproduce the historical result.

**Reasoning.** The proper object of review is the judgment and dispute before the Court. A procedural mismatch cannot be cured by assuming standing or substituting a different procurement program. A properly presented program must be examined through its objective, evidence, operation and burdens under the fictional governing law.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Identify the mismatch and jurisdictional or presentation limits precisely. If the merits are reached, distinguish the controlling fictional racial-classification standard from the real-world doctrine.

##### Barnes v. Gorman — punitive damages and statutory notice

**History and docket placement.** Carried forward from the tracker’s OT2001 list, which also treats the case as a landmark concerning remedies under funding conditions.

**Questions.** Do ADA Title II and §504 authorize punitive damages through their remedial cross-references? What notice do those provisions give covered recipients?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Reverse the punitive-damages award, preserving ordinary compensation for proved injury where authorized.

**Reasoning.** A valid access right and an available compensatory remedy do not establish punitive damages. The statutes’ cross-references and the notice supplied to covered recipients require a distinct remedial inquiry.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Identify the statutory chain supplying remedies and explain why it does or does not authorize this category of damages. Preserve the distinction between substantive coverage, compensatory relief and punitive awards.

##### Gonzaga University v. Doe — a protected interest and a proper §1983 defendant

**History and docket placement.** Carried forward from the tracker’s OT2001 list; linked to question P2.

**Questions.** Does FERPA create a specifically protected confidentiality interest enforceable through §1983? Does administrative oversight displace that remedy? Can the particular defendants satisfy the state-action and other requirements?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Reject the categorical no-right holding and preserve §1983 enforceability of the specifically protected interest, following the historical Stevens dissent. Remand the separate questions of state action, policy or practice, causation and remedy.

**Reasoning.** Administrative enforcement does not automatically displace §1983, but accepting federal funds does not by itself make a private university a state actor. Recognizing a statutory right therefore does not establish liability against the defendants in this case.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Identify the rights-creating language and address displacement under the applicable rule. Explain why this legislatively supplied §1983 route differs from *Sandoval*’s implied-action and regulatory questions. Do not assume a proper defendant or factual causation from the existence of a right.

##### Utah v. Evans — enumeration and imputation

**History and docket placement.** Carried forward from the tracker’s OT2001 list.

**Questions.** Does Utah have standing to challenge the census method? Does hot-deck imputation fill missing information about identifiable census units, or replace enumeration with prohibited sampling?

**Stone’s carried-forward recommendation.** Recognize Utah’s standing and affirm the judgment for the Census Bureau, rejecting the statutory and constitutional objections.

**Reasoning.** The older plan distinguishes filling missing information about particular census units from substituting sampling for enumeration. It treats that distinction as consistent with fictional *Wisconsin v. City of New York* and *Commerce v. House*.

**What the opinion must articulate.** Address standing separately; explain the method actually used and its fit with the statute and enumeration requirement. Do not infer the legality of every statistical method from approval of this one.
